### [2026-11-09 11:05] Assistant

I didn't run `/egm:egm-bootstrap`. That skill writes README contracts for code that already exists, and what you asked for is a review of your plan against the brief. Below I check your plan strictly against the PDF, worst problems first. I also saved a memory so future sessions treat the brief as the spec.

---

## 1. Problems in the flow you described

### A. RAGAS as the check step is the wrong tool (most serious)
- **It uses up your budget.** RAGAS faithfulness splits the answer into claims and then checks each claim with another LLM call. That adds roughly 3–10 extra Groq calls to every request. Free-tier requests per day (RPD) and tokens per minute (TPM) can't cover that, and the "per-minute limit halved" and "daily budget exhausted" scenarios get much worse.
- **It gives the wrong labels.** The brief grades answers as `correct / wrong / refused_correctly / refused_wrongly`. RAGAS gives scores between 0 and 1, and you would have to map those to labels in a way you could defend live. A small gold set of 15+ questions, each with an expected answer, an expected section and an "answerable or not" flag, gives you those labels directly.
- **Fix:** split checking into two parts.
  - **At runtime:** a deterministic check-citations tool. Every claim must carry a citation, every cited chunk ID must be one that was actually retrieved, and quoted numbers must appear in that chunk. It costs milliseconds and no tokens. Only call one cheap LLM judge when that check is inconclusive.
  - **Offline:** grade the Table 3 answers against your gold set. If you want, RAGAS can go here as an extra.

### B. Checking before sending conflicts with streaming
In your flow, generation finishes, then the check runs, then streaming starts. So time to first token (TTFT) equals the full generation time plus the check time, and the streaming adds nothing. The brief is pointing at exactly this trade-off. You have three options:
1. **Check everything, then send.** Safe, but TTFT is bad.
2. **Stream immediately and retract if the check fails.** This breaks "never send a wrong answer."
3. **Buffer one sentence at a time.** Stream each sentence once its citation passes the deterministic check.

I'd pick option 3 and make it the "one decision that shaped it most" for heading 1. Write down its cost: per-sentence checks catch fake citations but not subtle misreadings.

### C. The flow has no branch, so it fails "at least one branch or loop"
Your flow is a straight line. The brief also says it must "decide what to do," and that "a few loop." Add both:
- **A decide step with a branch:** answer, refuse because the papers don't say, or split a multi-part or cross-paper question into sub-questions.
- **A capped loop:** if retrieval confidence is low, rephrase the query and retrieve again, at most twice. This is also where "a few loop" shows up under load.

### D. The refusal path is missing
"If the papers do not say, the assistant must say so." You need a retrieval-score threshold plus an instruction telling the model to abstain. The `refused_correctly` and `refused_wrongly` grades only make sense if this path exists.

### E. The tool call needs to be explicit
To be safe, make at least one step a real model-driven tool call through Groq function calling. Good candidates are `search_papers` and `get_section`, with the check-citations tool from A as another. A retrieval function the code always runs on its own may not count as a tool call.

### F. The request manager needs more than a queue
- **Limit by tokens as well as requests.** TPM will probably bind before requests per minute (RPM) once retrieved context is included.
- **Count model calls, not user requests.** One user request turns into 3–6 model calls, and some turn into many more.
- **Adapt to Groq's live limit headers.** Read the `x-ratelimit-*` headers and `retry-after`. The brief says limits "can change without notice," so hard-coded numbers will fail.
- **Give every request a deadline, and cancel work when the user gives up.** Abandoned requests should not keep spending tokens. This drives the "given up %" column.
- **Track a daily budget.** It needs degraded modes: fewer chunks, no re-rank, a smaller model.

### G. Whole areas of the brief are missing from the plan
| Required | Status in your plan |
|---|---|
| Stand-in model service: slow, fails, sends 429, slows under load, streams, calibrated from real data | **Missing.** Build it as an OpenAI-compatible HTTP server so the pipeline only swaps `base_url`, and have it send Groq-style headers. |
| Load generator. Table 2a needs **five** scenarios: steady, burst, sustained overload, per-minute limit halved, daily budget exhausted | **Missing.** The brief's text says "three," but the table requires five. |
| `results.csv` with the exact columns, one row per request | **Missing** |
| Timestamps for each phase: queue wait, each call, retries, first token. Heading 4 needs waiting, working and retrying split out for one slow request | **Missing.** Build it in from day one, because you can't add it after the runs. |
| Retry policy plus **one observed case** where retrying made things worse (e.g. retry storm during the burst) | **Missing.** You have to actually cause and record it. |
| Cost per 1,000 answers | The free tier costs $0, so price it at Groq's paid per-token rates and say so. |
| Three or more retrieval settings (piece size, pieces fetched, re-rank) | Covered below |
| "Mislead a naive retriever": three ways, each with an example question | Covered below |
| Vaani feedback, raw conversation history, a two-page limit | Not mentioned. Keep this transcript; it counts. |
| Define "fast" with your own numbers, e.g. TTFT p95 under X s and give-up after Y s | Not stated |

### H. The question-understanding step costs an LLM call
Run it on a small, fast model, or with heuristics when budget is tight. On Groq, limits are set per model, so routing steps across models is a real design lever. It is also something to state openly in your write-up, since the brief forbids "tricks." I think routing is legitimate, but say it plainly.

---

## 2. Parent-child chunking: affordable to build, risky to send
Computing it is trivial with only three papers. The risk is the **token** cost:
- **Sizes vary a lot.** The Attention paper's sections are short. Moshi and the Dynamic Chunking (H-Net) paper have long top-level sections with many subsections, and a single "parent" could be several thousand tokens.
- **The limit is close.** As I last knew it, Groq free-tier TPM is around 6K–12K depending on the model; check your console. At that size, sending two or three parents in one request hits the per-minute limit by itself.

What I recommend:
- **Get section structure from the LaTeX source.** Use `arxiv.org/e-print/<id>v<N>`, not PDF text extraction. You get clean section and subsection boundaries, table captions, and citations like "Vaswani et al. §3.2.2, Table 3". It also pins the exact version the brief asks for.
- **Index children only.** Make them roughly 300–500 tokens at paragraph or subsection level, each carrying its section path.
- **Make expanding to the parent an option you test, not the default.** That gives you a natural Table 3: small children, k=8; medium children, k=4, plus re-rank; child plus a capped parent of ≤1.5K tokens. When near the limit, fall back to children only, lower k and no re-rank. That is your answer to "what you do with retrieval when the limit is close."
- **Embed locally.** As far as I know, Groq doesn't serve embedding models; check that. Use a hybrid of BM25 and a small local embedding model. Exact terms like `d_model`, "Mimi", "H-Net" and "dynamic chunking" need lexical matching.

Candidates for "mislead a naive retriever":
- **The same words in all three papers.** "Attention" or "Transformer" appear everywhere, since Moshi and H-Net both use Transformers. Example: "What attention variant does the paper use?"
- **Answers that live in tables.** Results and hyperparameters sit in tables, which chunk badly. Example: "What BLEU did the big model get on EN-DE?"
- **Reference lists that match the query.** Bibliographies and related-work sections describe other papers. Example: "What does Moshi say about Whisper?" may retrieve the reference list.
- **"Chunking" means two things.** H-Net's topic is dynamic chunking, which collides with the word's use in RAG.

---

## 3. Plan for testing Groq's limits (Table 1)
Your daily budget is shared by everything you run, so test limits **early, cheaply and with logs**. Save every raw response, headers included, to `probe_raw.jsonl`.

| Phase | What | Fills | Rough cost |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | Read the Groq rate-limit docs and your console's limits page for 2–3 candidate models | "Value you found" from docs | 0 |
| 1 | One request per model with `max_tokens=1`, logging all `x-ratelimit-*` headers (as I recall, `limit-requests` there is RPD and `limit-tokens` is TPM) | RPD, TPM, confidence from headers | ~3 req |
| 2 | Latency distribution: ~60–100 streamed requests below RPM, at three input sizes (~200, ~1.5K, ~4K tokens) with fixed output, across two time windows | TTFT and total latency spread (stand-in calibration) | ~100 req, ~150K tokens |
| 3 | RPM: send RPM+5 tiny requests within 10 s; record which one gets the first 429, the body, and `retry-after`. Then send one tiny request every 2 s until it succeeds | RPM, "what it returns", "time until it recovers" | ~50 req |
| 4 | TPM: send ~4K-token prompts back to back until 429; confirm the error names tokens rather than requests | TPM, recovery time | ~5 req, ~25K tokens |
| 5 | Concurrency: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 parallel tiny streams; watch for rising latency and for 429 or 5xx errors | "Requests in flight" | ~31 req |
| 6 | Daily limit: limits are per model, so exhaust the daily cap on one cheap model you don't otherwise use, to see the real per-day 429 and reset time without spending your main model's budget. Do it **near the end of a day**. | RPD and TPD behaviour, real "daily exhausted" response | That model's whole budget |

Then **calibrate the stand-in** from phases 2–5. Fit a latency distribution (lognormal plus a heavy tail), a tokens-per-second rate, and the measured 429 behaviour, and add load-dependent slowdown. Write down where it differs most from the real service. A likely candidate: the stand-in's slowdown under load is your own model, while Groq's is hidden.

---

## 4. Where to go next
Revised flow: **admission and budget control → understand and route (small model) → retrieve (tool; capped retry loop) → decide (answer, refuse or split) → draft (large model, cites chunk IDs) → per-sentence citation check (tool), streamed → log one CSV row.**

If you want, I can run the `architecture` skill on this next to write it up as an ADR with the trade-offs. After that I'd look at the repo directory, which I haven't seen yet, to see what already exists.

Written for: you, as your working design record. It isn't the 2-page write-up; it gives you material for it.

I wrote [docs/adr/0001-relay-pipeline-architecture.md](docs/adr/0001-relay-pipeline-architecture.md). It compares four designs and recommends **Option C**: a staged pipeline that checks each sentence's citations before streaming it, with a zero-token fallback.

**The four options:**
- **A, your original plan:** it doesn't meet the brief. It has no branch or loop and no refusal path. Putting RAGAS on every request adds about 3–10 extra calls per request. Section-level parent chunks can use a whole minute's token limit in one request.
- **B, check the whole answer, then send:** safe, but time to first token is poor and streaming adds nothing.
- **C, the recommendation:** before each sentence goes out, a code check confirms it has a citation, the cited chunk was actually retrieved, and every number appears in that chunk. This costs no tokens. **What it costs in quality:** it catches made-up citations and numbers, but not a correctly cited sentence that misreads its source. That gap gets measured offline with your question set.
- **D, no check at all:** rejected, but kept as a measured baseline. Comparing it with C gives you a number nobody could guess without running it.

**What the ADR settles:**
- **Chunking:** your parent-child idea stays, changed. Only the small child chunks are searched. Pulling in the parent section becomes one of the retrieval settings you test, and it is capped at 1,500 tokens.
- **Behaviour at the limits:** a ladder of five levels (L0–L4), each with a named trigger and the users it affects. This answers your write-up headings 5 and 7. When the daily budget is gone, people get cited passages from local search with a note on when full answers resume.
- **Retries:** a policy plus a planned experiment that shows naive retries making a burst worse (heading 6).
- **Stand-in service:** how it's built, calibrated from your measurements, and where it's least like Groq.
- **Logging:** the exact `results.csv` columns, plus a per-step event log for heading 4.
- **Groq testing:** a 7-phase plan, with the daily-limit test run on a separate model you don't otherwise use.

**Things to check rather than trust:**
- I wrote the Groq model names and "Groq serves no embedding models" from memory. Probe phases 0–1 confirm both.
- The speed targets (first token p95 ≤ 3 s, total ≤ 15 s) are placeholders. Set them once you've measured real latency.
- The ADR asks you to say in the write-up that you split steps across two models, since each has its own limits. That's my reading of the brief's rule against getting around limits, not something the brief says. Decide that one yourself.

**Open decision for you:** what `answer_grade` to record for stand-in rows. The stand-in's answers are copied sentences, so grading them doesn't measure real model quality.

**Before writing any code:** run `git init` inside the project folder and add `.env` to `.gitignore`. Right now the git repository is your whole home folder.

## Test Groq first or after building the RAG?

**Test first, in stages.**

1. **The brief sets the order.** "First, send a modest number of real requests to learn how the real service behaves... Then run your pipeline." Reviewers will look for that sequence in your raw data.
2. **Your design numbers depend on it.** You need the real per-minute token limit to choose how many chunks to fetch and how big they can be. You need it to know whether the token limit hits before the request limit. You need it to set the thresholds for each level of the fallback ladder. You also need real model names and limits before routing work between two models makes sense. Building the RAG first means guessing all of these and redoing work.
3. **The daily limit resets.** Spending part of today's budget on probes costs nothing on later days, when you'll want the budget for test questions and real load runs.
4. **Probes don't need the pipeline.** Plain test prompts at the right sizes (~200, ~1.5K, ~4K tokens) produce the same latency and limit data.

**What to hold back:**
- **Daily-limit exhaustion (phase 6):** run it at the end of a day, on a model you don't otherwise use.
- **One quick header check at the very end:** this shows whether limits changed during your four days. The brief warns they can.

**Suggested order:**
1. Probe phases 0–2 today, plus phases 3–5. They're cheap.
2. Build the stand-in from that data while you build the pipeline.
3. Run the questions and real load tests.
4. Exhaust the daily limit at the end of a day.

If you want, I can write the probe script next. It would log every raw response, headers included, to `probe_raw.jsonl`. Before it runs anything that spends your budget, I'd need you to confirm which models to test.

### [2026-11-09 11:57] Assistant

The probe script is built and the dry run of every phase worked. I haven't sent any requests yet because `.env` still has no Groq key.

**Built:**
- A Python 3.11 virtual environment in `.venv`, and [requirements.txt](requirements.txt) with `httpx` and `python-dotenv`.
- [probe/groq_probe.py](probe/groq_probe.py), with one command per probe phase. Each request becomes one JSON line in `results/probe_raw.jsonl`. The line has the full response headers, any 429 error body, our timing, and Groq's own usage and timing fields. The key is never printed or logged.
  - Every prompt starts with a unique random string, because Groq doesn't count cached tokens toward limits.
  - It times the first reasoning token and the first answer token separately.
  - The daily-limit command won't run without `--confirm-exhaust`.
- The ADR now records the test-first schedule, your model choices, what the Groq docs say, and the daily-refill risk.

**Today's estimated spend from the dry run, all on `gpt-oss-20b`:**
- Latency, 45 requests: about 89K tokens, about 14 minutes.
- Requests-per-minute test: up to 35 requests, plus recovery checks.
- Tokens-per-minute test: up to 6 requests of about 3.6K tokens each.
- Concurrency: 31 requests.

The latency phase could also run on `gpt-oss-120b`, the answer-writing model, since the stand-in should be calibrated against it.

**Two things I need from you:**
1. **Paste your key into `.env`** as `GROQ_API_KEY=gsk_...`. Git ignores that file, and I checked that.
2. **Open console.groq.com/settings/limits and paste the free-tier rows** for `gpt-oss-20b`, `gpt-oss-120b` and `llama-3.1-8b-instant`. Groq's response headers don't report requests per minute or tokens per day. The console is the "from documentation" source for those columns of Table 1, and the script uses them to pace requests.

Once I have the key, I'll run the cheap first phase: one tiny request per model, three in total. The `x-ratelimit-reset-requests` values from that run will give a first sign of whether the daily limit refills gradually, before anything is exhausted.

### [2026-11-09 13:29] Assistant

## Gap 3: what happens after the daily budget runs out

### The question behind it

The brief asks for two things here. Table 1 needs **"Time until it recovers"**. Heading 7 needs **"At 3pm the daily budget is gone. Describe the reply a waiting person receives."**

Both depend on one unknown: **once the daily budget is gone, how does it come back?** We've only measured the answer for the per-minute limits.

### What we know, and what we're assuming

For **requests per day**, the headers settle it. `reset-requests` was exactly (requests used) × 86.4 s every time we looked, on three models. The request budget refills continuously, like a bucket filling at a steady rate:

```
1,000 requests / 86,400 s  =  one request back every 86.4 s
```

For **tokens per day**, we know nothing. No header reports it, which is exactly why tonight's test targets tokens. When I say "it probably refills the same way", that's an assumption carried over from the request budget.

### Three possibilities, and why they matter

| Hypothesis | How capacity returns after the daily 429 | What the person sees at 3pm |
|---|---|---|
| **H1: continuous refill** (as the request budget does) | Capacity drips back at TPD ÷ 86,400 tokens/s, starting immediately | A small trickle of capacity exists. A small request gets through soon; a big one waits longer. |
| **H2: fixed reset** (e.g. midnight UTC) | Nothing passes until the reset, whatever the request size | Nothing works until a fixed time we can name ("full answers resume at 05:30 IST") |
| **H3: rolling 24 h window** | Tokens come back exactly 24 h after they were spent | Nothing passes for hours. Capacity then returns in the same pattern it was spent in. |

**Under H1, request size sets the wait.** If TPD were 200K (the paid plan's figure; ours is unknown), the refill is about 2.3 tokens/s:

```
tiny request   (~100 tokens)   →  100 / 2.3   ≈ 43 s
router call    (~600 tokens)   →  600 / 2.3   ≈ 4.3 min
drafting call  (~2,900 tokens) →  2,900 / 2.3 ≈ 21 min
```

Remember that Groq counts `max_tokens` in the requested amount, so a generous `max_tokens` lengthens the wait too.

**So "the daily budget is gone" may not be all-or-nothing.** Under H1, at 3pm you'd still have about one full answer every 20 minutes, or many small calls. That gives you real design choices:
- Do you spend the trickle on the person who's waited longest?
- Do you spend it only on cheap router calls and serve everyone extractive answers?
- Do you deliberately leave it unused?

Under H2 or H3, those choices don't exist.

**It also changes what the message says.** Under H2 you can say "back at 05:30". Under H1 there's no single reset time. The honest message becomes "full answers are limited right now; your next full answer could be ready in about N minutes", with N computed from the deficit and the refill rate.

### Why the script can't answer this today

`phase_daily` stops at the first per-day 429. We'd see **what** the 429 says, but not **when capacity actually returns**. That's half the Table 1 row and none of the evidence for heading 7.

### What the fix does

After the first per-day 429, the script keeps probing for about 30–40 minutes on two schedules:
- **A tiny request every 60 s.** This catches early capacity under H1 (the ~43 s case).
- **A full-size request (~2,900 tokens) every 5 min.** This catches the ~21-minute case and shows whether wait time scales with size.

Every probe is logged with its time since the first per-day 429.

**What it costs:** a rejected call returns in ~55 ms and isn't counted (we measured that for the per-minute 429). Calls that succeed only use budget on `qwen`, which is sacrificial anyway. It takes about 30–40 extra minutes of wall time, in the background. The pipeline models are untouched.

### How the results tell the hypotheses apart

| Observation | Points to |
|---|---|
| The tiny probe succeeds within ~1 min; the large one takes far longer | **H1.** Recovery depends on request size. |
| Nothing succeeds, but the 429's "try again in" stays fixed or counts down to one clock time | **H2** |
| Nothing succeeds, and "try again in" is measured in hours | **H3** |

### A second check: the 429's own wait time

The per-minute 429 told us the exact wait ("Please try again in 3.4125s"). The per-day 429 will probably state one too. Under H1 it should equal (tokens requested − tokens remaining) ÷ refill rate. The polling checks whether that stated wait is **true**, instead of us just trusting what the service says. That's the brief's "from what it sends back when you reach one", done properly.

### Where it feeds into the write-up

- **Table 1, "Time until it recovers":** measured for the daily limit, not inferred.
- **Heading 5:** who gets the trickle at the per-day limit, and why.
- **Heading 7:** the actual 3pm message, with a wait you can defend.
- **Heading 8 ("least certain assumption"):** if we skipped this, "the token budget refills like the request budget" would be the answer, and the honest admission would be that if it's wrong, the 3pm message promises a time that doesn't happen.
- **The stand-in:** its daily-limit behaviour, used in the "daily budget exhausted" row of Table 2a, gets calibrated to whichever pattern Groq actually follows.

Should I add this polling to the daily test before tonight? It's a contained change to `phase_daily`, and I'll dry-run it before anything spends budget.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - The Groq OpenAI-compatible API (`https://api.groq.com/openai/v1/chat/completions`), with SSE streaming.
   - Groq rate-limit headers:
     - `x-ratelimit-limit-requests` gives RPD; `x-ratelimit-limit-tokens` gives TPM.
     - `remaining-*` and `reset-*` follow the same split.

   - `max_tokens` is reserved against TPM up front and includes reasoning tokens.
   - gpt-oss reasoning tokens with `reasoning_effort=low`.
   - Groq `usage.queue_time`, `prompt_time`, `completion_time`, `total_time`.
   - A nonce per prompt to defeat caching ("Cached tokens do not count towards your rate limits").
   - The ADR (Architecture Decision Record) format.
   - Option C: a per-sentence deterministic citation gate (the `check_citations` tool), a conditional LLM judge, and a zero-token extractive fallback.

what do you suggest to do with gap 3? should we send all the requests now and monitor when it comes back??

### [2026-11-09 17:03] Assistant

**Yes, run it now instead of at the end of the day.** Two conditions: pace the requests rather than firing them all at once, and use only `qwen`.

### Why now is better than end of day

- **Running late was only there to protect the pipeline models.** The daily test runs on `qwen`, and the headers show each model has its own daily counters. So `gpt-oss-20b` and `120b` aren't touched whenever we run it.
- **Recovery takes hours to watch, possibly.** If the budget refills continuously, we'll know within about 30 minutes. If it resets at a fixed time or after a rolling 24 hours, we need hours of watching. Started at end of day, that part happens while you're asleep or the laptop is closed.
- **Tokens per day is a design input.** It sets how many full answers we can afford per day. That sizes `max_tokens`, how many pieces we fetch, and the budget for the 15+ question evaluation. Better to know it today.

### Why "all at once" doesn't work

The per-minute limit caps us at 8,000 tokens a minute. A burst would just hit per-minute 429s and never reach the daily limit. The fastest way to the daily limit is a steady ~7,600 tokens a minute:

| If tokens per day is | Time to use it up |
|---|---|
| 200K | ~26 min |
| 500K | ~66 min |
| Not used up within the 180 min cap | We report a lower bound: "tokens per day > ~1.3M" |

### The run, in three stages

1. **Use up the budget.** Send large requests to `qwen`, paced to the per-minute limit.
2. **Close watch for 40 minutes after the first daily 429.**
   - A tiny request every 60 s.
   - A large request every 5 min.
   - One probe at exactly the wait the 429 tells us, to check whether Groq's stated wait is true.
3. **Slow watch, up to about 6 hours or until a large request succeeds.** One tiny and one large request every 15 min. This catches the fixed-reset and 24-hour cases.

Rejected calls don't use budget, so stages 2 and 3 cost almost nothing. The only real cost is the `qwen` budget, which is the one we set aside to use up.

### Two additions worth making

- **Prove the budgets are separate per model.** Send one tiny `gpt-oss-20b` call before the run and one after, and check its daily counter didn't drop. That costs 2 requests. If the counters did turn out to be shared, it would change everything about planning the daily budget.
- **Find out whether the daily limit charges `max_tokens` or what's actually used.** Make some of the stage 1 requests ask for a one-word answer with `max_tokens` around 300. The daily 429 should report "Used N", like the per-minute one did. We compare N with the two possible totals: `max_tokens` reserved, or tokens actually used. This tells us whether a generous `max_tokens` on the drafter also uses up the daily budget, not just the per-minute one. One caveat: if `qwen` writes out its reasoning and fills the 300 tokens, this check is void, and I'll say so.

### What it won't catch

- **Stage 3 needs the laptop awake and online.** If it sleeps, polling stops, but everything recorded up to then is kept.
- **The result comes from `qwen`.** Whether `gpt-oss` behaves the same is an assumption, so it belongs in heading 8 (what I'm unsure of).

### Your call

This changes your earlier "at the end of my day" decision, so it's yours to make. If you say go, I'll:
1. Add stages 2 and 3 and the `max_tokens` check to `phase_daily`.
2. Dry-run it and show you the exact request and token cost.
3. Start it in the background once you OK the numbers, and record the timing change in the ADR as your decision.

**The console agrees with everything we measured.** RPM 30, RPD 1K and TPM 8K match the headers and the 429 messages. Most of Table 1 now has two independent sources each, which supports "High" confidence.

**It also shows a problem: tokens per day is 200K, and that's the daily limit that will actually stop us.**
- A full answer reserves about 3.6K tokens on `gpt-oss-120b` (~3K prompt plus ~600 `max_tokens`). 200K buys roughly **55 full answers a day**. The 1,000-requests-a-day limit will never come into play.
- Today's probing already used about 82K of the 120b budget and 70K of the 20b budget.
- The quality evaluation the brief requires is at least 15 questions × at least 3 retrieval settings, so 45+ drafts or about 160K tokens. That's nearly a full day's budget by itself.
- The real load-run rows will need a day of their own.
- **So we need a daily budget plan before we build the evaluation or the load runs.** It decides which day spends tokens on what. I've marked it as blocking in the ADR (action item B8, the daily budget plan).

**It makes tonight's test simpler.** Using up 200K at about 7,600 tokens a minute takes roughly **26 minutes**. The test now checks the console figure and measures recovery, rather than discovering the limit.

If tokens refill the way requests do, at about 2.3 tokens a second, then after the limit is hit:
- a tiny request gets through after about 43 s;
- a full draft gets through after about 21 min.

That recovery is what Gap 3 is about.

**One decision before tonight:** should I add the recovery watching to the daily test? That's the close watch for 40 minutes, then a check every 15 minutes, plus the check on whether `max_tokens` counts against the daily limit. It doesn't use any budget until you run it, and I'll do a dry run first. If you want the slow watch overnight, keep the laptop awake and online.

## 1. Recovery watching: added, nothing sent yet

[probe/groq_probe.py](probe/groq_probe.py) `phase_daily` now runs in three stages, and [probe/analyze_probe.py](probe/analyze_probe.py) reports on all of them.

1. **Use up the budget.** About 63 large requests to `qwen`, roughly 26 min. Every second request asks for a one-word answer with `max_tokens=600`. When the daily limit hits, the refusal says "Used N". We compare N with two totals: tokens actually used, and tokens reserved through `max_tokens`. That tells us which one the daily budget charges.
2. **Drain the leftovers.** I changed the plan here. When the first large request is refused, a few thousand tokens are usually still in the budget. Tiny requests would get through on those, which would look like refill when it isn't. So the script sends tiny requests until a tiny one is refused too. Recovery is timed from that point.
3. **Watch the refill.** A large request every 60 s, and a tiny one in between until a tiny gets through. After 40 minutes, one of each every 15 min, for up to 6 h. Each refusal's "Used N" is a free reading of the budget, so the analyzer fits the refill rate from the slope of those readings. It also probes at exactly the wait each refusal states, to check that Groq's stated wait is accurate.

`--check-model openai/gpt-oss-20b` sends one tiny request before the run and one after, which shows whether each model has its own daily budget. Those two requests use about 200 tokens of 20b's budget.

**Checks done (no requests sent):**
- The new parser reads our two real 429 messages correctly: Limit, Used, Requested, and the 2 s and 3.4125 s waits.
- It also reads a made-up daily 429 correctly.
- The dry run gives ~63 requests, ~26 min, and ~198K `qwen` tokens.

Tonight's command is in [CLAUDE.md](CLAUDE.md). Keep the laptop awake for the 6-hour watch.

## 2. The budget plan: how I'd approach it

Plan in **tokens per model per day**. Requests per day don't matter.
- The 20b and 120b each have their own 200K a day, refilling at about 8.3K an hour.
- The project has about 4 days, so roughly 800K per model in total. Day 1 has already used ~82K on the 120b.

**Step 1: measure what an answer costs before planning around it.** The 3.6K I quoted is an estimate. Once the pipeline runs against the stand-in, run 3 real questions at each retrieval setting. That gives the real router and drafter tokens per answer, and makes every later line of the plan arithmetic.

**Step 2: list everything the brief needs real calls for, and cost each item.**

| Item | Real calls | Rough cost on 120b |
|---|---|---|
| Retrieval comparison: ≥15 questions × ≥3 settings | ~45 answers | ~160K. Close to a full day. |
| Table 2a real rows: steady and burst at least | ~30 answers | ~110K, plus rejected calls, which are free |
| "Daily budget exhausted" as a real row | Exhausting the 120b | Its whole day, so it has to be the last evening |
| Development smoke tests | ~10–20 answers | ~50K |
| Final-day header recheck (gap 8) | 2 tiny | ~0 |

**Step 3: pull the cost levers before spending anything.**
- **Do development against the stand-in only.**
- **Measure retrieval quality without the model.** Whether the right passage was retrieved can be checked against labelled answers with no Groq calls at all. Real drafts are then needed only for answer quality, tokens, and first-token time.
- **Choose `max_tokens` from data**, because it may be charged against the daily budget. Tonight's test tells us.
- **Prompt caching:** Groq says cached tokens don't count against limits. A stable system prompt might be cheaper. It's documented Groq behaviour rather than a trick, but it would make load runs look better than they are, so we'd state it openly. Worth checking.

**Step 4: keep a ledger with a guard.**
- A small `budget.py` totals real tokens per model per day from the raw files.
- Every real run prints its projected cost in its dry run and refuses to start if that would exceed the day's planned share.
- Keep about 15% in reserve for reruns.

**A draft calendar** (assuming the deadline is Nov 13, four days from today):

| Day | Plan |
|---|---|
| Nov 9 | Probes (done), then tonight's `qwen` daily test |
| Nov 10 | Ingestion, pipeline and stand-in; measure cost per answer; start the retrieval comparison |
| Nov 11 | Finish the retrieval comparison; real steady and burst runs |
| Nov 12 | Fix anything that went wrong; header recheck; exhaust the 120b in the evening for the real daily-exhausted row; write-up |

You need to make two decisions here:
- **Which Table 2a rows are real.** I recommend steady and burst real, and sustained overload and per-minute-limit-halved on the stand-in. The halved limit can't be real, because we can't change Groq's limit.
- **Whether the daily-exhausted row is real on the 120b on the last night, or comes from the stand-in.** The stand-in version would be calibrated from tonight's `qwen` run.

| **7. Limit tests ran only on the 20b** | No dedicated test. Tonight's run covers the daily limit on a third model. The real burst run on the 120b will hit its per-minute limit naturally, giving that 429 for free. Table 1's confidence column says this. | 0 |

### [2026-11-09 17:31] Assistant

I've recorded your decisions in the ADR: steady and burst run on real Groq, sustained overload and per-minute-limit-halved on the stand-in, plus your answers on gaps 5, 6 and 8. I also copied the brief PDF into the repo root, which was still outstanding.

## The daily-exhausted row, explained

Table 2a needs a row for "daily budget exhausted", marked real or stand-in. It measures what people experience once the day's budget is gone. There are two ways to fill it:

| | Real (last evening, Nov 12) | Stand-in |
|---|---|---|
| How | Run the real pipeline on Groq until `gpt-oss-120b`'s daily budget runs out. Keep sending questions, and record what people get and how fast. | Set the stand-in's daily budget to empty, using refill behaviour calibrated from tonight's `qwen` test. Run questions against it. |
| Cost | The 120b's whole remaining budget that day. It has to run last, because nothing real can run after it. | No Groq budget. Can be repeated as often as we like. |
| Strength | Shows Groq's real behaviour at the limit with our pipeline in the loop. Reviewers can see it isn't simulated. | Safe and repeatable, but a reviewer can ask how accurate the stand-in's version of the daily limit is. |

**I recommend real, folded into the steady and burst runs.** Those runs spend 120b tokens anyway, so run them on the last evening and keep going until the budget is empty. What follows becomes the daily-exhausted row. We pay once and get three real rows, with the stand-in version as a fallback if something breaks. The catch is that the real runs then can't happen any earlier, which squeezes the schedule.

## Gap 4: the PDF actually requires probe rows

I reread it. Two lines decide it:
- *"one row per request"*. Probe calls are requests.
- *"Every figure in the tables must be computable from this file … reviewers … recompute them from your raw file."* That includes Table 1.

So if `results.csv` held only pipeline answers, the reviewers couldn't recompute Table 1, which breaks the PDF. Here is what sticking strictly to the PDF means as I read it:
- Exactly the 12 columns and exactly the Table 1 layout, with nothing added.
- The probe requests go in as rows, for example `scenario=probe_rpm`, with status `ok` or `rate_limited`.
- RPM, TPM, tokens per day (after tonight), requests in flight, and recovery time can all then be counted from rows.

**The PDF doesn't settle two details:**
- **Probe rows have no answer to grade.** The PDF allows only four grades, so I'd leave `answer_grade` empty on those rows and say why in the write-up.
- **Requests per day can't be recomputed.** We never hit it, so its value comes only from the headers and the console. Table 1's "How you found it" column would say exactly that.

Does that match what you meant?

## Gap 6: tonight's second time window

You're right that we have spare tokens, and they're worth spending. The budget can't hold more than 200K and refills about 8.3K an hour, so tokens left unspent tonight are simply lost by morning. I suggest repeating this afternoon's run exactly, so the two windows compare like for like.

| Run | Requests | Tokens | Time |
|---|---|---|---|
| `gpt-oss-120b`: small, medium and large, 15 each | 45 | ~70–89K (the dry run overestimates) | ~14 min |
| `gpt-oss-20b`: small | 15 | ~6.5K | ~1 min |

Run these before the `qwen` daily test. The 120b refills overnight.

## Gap 7, explained

We measured what happens at the per-minute limits by hitting them, but **only on `gpt-oss-20b`**, the router. The drafter, `gpt-oss-120b`, never hit a limit. Its limit values are confirmed by the headers and the console. Its behaviour at the limit is not: the 429 message, the stated wait, how fast it recovers. We're assuming it matches the 20b's.

**What I recommend:** no extra test. The real burst run on the 120b will hit its per-minute limit on its own, which gives us a real 429 from it for free. Table 1 then says "seen on both models" instead of "seen on 20b, assumed for 120b".

## Planning the budget for the product

Agreed: the brief says *"running out is part of the problem"*, and headings 5 and 7 are about exactly that. So there are two layers, sharing one piece of code.

**The numbers that matter** (estimated until we measure the real cost per answer):

| | |
|---|---|
| Full answers that fit in a full budget | ~55 a day (200K ÷ ~3.6K) |
| Speed limit within a burst | ~2.2 full answers a minute (TPM 8K) |
| Long-run pace once the stored budget is spent | **~2.3 full answers an hour** (the refill rate) |
| Router (20b, its own 200K) | ~260 routings a day |

So the product can handle one burst of about 55 people well. After that it can give each person a full answer only about every 26 minutes. That limits capacity far more than the 1,000 requests a day.

**What the product's budget manager would do:**
1. **Track the budget live.** Read the per-minute limit and requests left from every response's headers. Groq never reports tokens per day, so keep our own running count, corrected whenever a daily 429 reports "Used N". Read the limits live rather than hard-coding them, because the brief says they *"can change without notice"*.
2. **Give each request a token allowance by tier:**
   - *Full:* ~3.6K tokens on the 120b.
   - *Lean:* fewer passages and a smaller `max_tokens`, or drafted on the 20b.
   - *Extractive:* cited passages quoted directly, with no drafter tokens.

   The tier depends on how much budget is left. That answers heading 5 (who gets a weaker answer) and the retrieval question (what retrieval does when the limit is close).
3. **Pace the day.** Keep part of the drafter's budget in reserve so a morning burst can't leave the afternoon with nothing.
4. **Cache answers.** The brief says bursts come *"right after something goes wrong for many users at once"*, so many people ask the same question. A cached, cited answer costs no tokens and almost no time. This is our own cache, so it isn't a trick to get around Groq's limits.
5. **Never turn anyone away.** If a person's wait would run past their patience, send the extractive answer immediately instead of dropping them.
6. **Report cost for Table 2b.** At Groq's list prices, a full answer costs about $0.0007, so roughly **$0.70 per 1,000 answers**. That's an estimate until we measure.

**I'd leave one option out:** using `qwen` as a third drafter. It's legal per model, but its only purpose would be extra budget, which reads as the kind of *"trick to get around its limits"* the brief forbids.

### Decisions I need from you
1. **The daily-exhausted row:** real on the last evening, folded into the steady and burst runs (my recommendation), or stand-in?
2. **The answer cache:** yes (my recommendation) or no?
3. **The 20b as a cheaper fallback drafter**, clearly labelled as a weaker answer: yes or no?
4. **How big the daily reserve is.** I'd suggest about 20% of the drafter's budget, which is about 11 full answers held back for the afternoon.

Once you answer, I'll write the budget-plan section of the ADR. The build-time ledger and the product's budget manager then become one shared component.

### Five details I filled in, which need your OK
They're in the ADR marked as my proposals:

- **(a) How the reserve is released.** Below 40%, the reserve is released evenly until 18:00 local time. That also lets the last-evening run reach a truly empty budget for the daily-exhausted row.
- **(b) How cached answers appear in the CSV.** A cache hit is recorded as `retrieval_setting=cache` with 0 tokens, so the hit rate can be counted. Load runs use a mixed set of questions with a stated repeat rate, so the cache doesn't look better than it is.
- **(c) The router has first claim on the 20b.** Shorter 20b answers stop while the 20b is below 25%, because the router and extractive answers depend on it.
- **(d) Evaluation runs never get downgraded.** If the budget can't cover an evaluation run, it refuses to start instead of comparing a mix of full and shorter answers.
- **(e) The 3pm message gives a time.** The budget refills gradually, so there's no "back at midnight" moment. The message says "next full answer in about N min" instead.

### [2026-11-09 18:12] Assistant

**Start now, but on the parts that don't depend on tonight's results.** Most of the system doesn't depend on them:

| Waits for tonight's runs | Can start now |
|---|---|
| The stand-in's daily-limit behaviour (from the `qwen` test) | Paper ingestion and chunking, retrieval, the evaluation questions |
| A small adjustment to latency calibration (evening window) | The Groq client, the budget ledger, the rate limiter, the pipeline stages |

So here's the whole system, revised in light of what we measured. Most of it is already in the ADR. What follows is **what the measurements changed** and the decisions still open.

---

## 1. What the measurements told us about the design

**a) Tokens decide everything; latency mostly doesn't.**
- The drafter manages about 55 full answers a day, and **~2.2 full answers a minute** within a burst (TPM 8K).
- A larger prompt costs only ~200 ms but uses 10× the budget.
- So every design choice gets judged on **tokens per answer first**.

**b) What a burst actually looks like.** Say 30 people arrive within 10 seconds:
- Only ~2 get a full 120b answer right away. After that, one more becomes possible every ~27 s.
- People's median patience is 20 s, so most would give up waiting.
- Bursts are therefore served by the **answer cache**, then **Lean answers on the 20b**, then the instant **extractive answer**. Waiting in the queue for a full answer is the last resort.
- This is the real answer to heading 5 at the per-minute limit, and it comes straight from the measured TPM.

**c) One draft per request, at most.** The ADR's DECOMPOSE branch fed sub-questions back through drafting, which multiplies tokens. **I propose:**
- Sub-questions only drive *retrieval*. The pieces they find are combined, and there's a single draft.
- At most one regeneration when the citation gate fails.
- Never more than 2 drafter calls per request.

**d) The per-sentence gate helps mainly in the slow tail.** You chose this, and it stays. But what it buys is different from what we expected:
- Groq generates at ~476 tokens/s, so a 300-token answer is complete ~0.6 s after its first token. **At the median**, checking the whole answer before sending would cost only ~0.6 s more than checking per sentence.
- In the **7% of responses that trickle at ~70 tokens/s**, the same answer takes ~4.3 s. There, sending sentence by sentence saves ~4 s.
- So the gate's value shows up at **p95, not p50**. The ADR's fallback trigger compares p50, which would make the gate look worse than it is. **I propose comparing p95 as well.**

**e) No hedged requests.** Sending a duplicate request to dodge Groq's queue delays is the standard fix for slow-for-no-reason calls. On this budget it doubles token cost. Rejected, and worth a sentence in the write-up.

**f) Expected speed, built up from measured parts:**
- p50 first answer token ≈ router (~0.4 s, 20b) + local retrieval (~0.1–0.3 s) + drafter first token (~0.6 s) ≈ **1.1–1.6 s**.
- At p95, a Groq queue episode adds ~2 s, so ≈ **3.5–4 s**.
- Total time p95 ≈ **5–6 s**, driven by the trickle.

**I propose new "fast" targets:** first token p95 ≤ 4,000 ms (the ADR guessed 3,000) and total p95 ≤ 8,000 ms (it guessed 15,000). Both now come from the data.

## 2. The system

```
question
   │
[0 Admission]   cache lookup ─hit─► cached cited answer (0 tokens)
   │            budget ledger picks the tier: Full / Lean / Extractive
   │            estimated wait > patience? ─► extractive now
[1 Route]       gpt-oss-20b tool call: search_papers | refuse_out_of_scope   (~450 tokens)
[2 Retrieve]    local hybrid BM25 + embeddings (+ re-rank), loop ≤2 if weak;
                sub-questions retrieval-only
[3 Decide]      ANSWER | REFUSE (papers don't say) | EXTRACTIVE (low budget)
[4 Draft]       Full: 120b · Lean: 20b · streamed, cites [paper §section]
[5 Gate]        per sentence: has a citation, cites a retrieved piece, its numbers appear in that piece
[6 Emit/log]    stream to user · trace.jsonl · pipeline_rows.csv · cache store
```

**One Groq client, two back ends.** The stand-in is a separate OpenAI-compatible HTTP server, so switching between real and stand-in is just a change of `base_url`. It copies what we measured:
- latency follows the fitted distribution, with queue delays that hit requests arriving together at the same time;
- 7% of responses trickle;
- the 429 has Groq's exact wording, returns in ~55 ms, and rejected calls aren't charged;
- budgets refill continuously, and `max_tokens` is reserved up front;
- daily recovery will follow whatever tonight shows.

## 3. Suggested repo layout and build order

| # | Component | Groq tokens | Depends on |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `corpus/`: fetch the LaTeX source at exact versions, split into sections, chunk with section paths | 0 | — |
| 2 | `relay/retrieval/`: BM25 + embeddings + fusion + optional re-rank | 0 | 1 |
| 3 | `eval/gold.yaml`: **your** ≥15 questions (see below) | 0 | 1 (to label the expected sections) |
| 4 | `relay/llm/`: async streaming client, budget ledger, rate limiter | 0 | — |
| 5 | `standin/`: server | 0 | tonight's daily data, for the daily bucket only |
| 6 | `relay/pipeline/`: stages 0–6, tiers, cache, gate | 0 (developed against the stand-in) | 2, 4, 5 |
| 7 | `loadgen/` plus `tables.py` | 0 | 6 |
| 8 | Real: smoke test to measure cost per answer, then evaluation, then the last-evening runs | budgeted | everything above |

You can measure retrieval quality per setting (did the right section come back?) **with no Groq calls at all**, once the gold questions are labelled. That lets us drop poor settings before spending any drafter tokens on them.

## 4. Decisions I need from you to start

1. **Evaluation questions are yours to write.** The brief says *"15 questions you wrote yourself"* and asks what in the submission is distinctly yours. I'd suggest about 20:
   - ≥5 the papers can't answer;
   - ≥3 that need two papers;
   - ≥3 asking for a number or table value;
   - a few designed to fool a naive retriever, which also gives you material for that heading.

   I can review them for coverage, but I shouldn't write them.
2. **The embedding stack.** I recommend **fastembed** (ONNX, small, installs easily on Windows; it includes both an embedder and a re-ranker). The alternative, sentence-transformers, pulls in the whole of PyTorch, which is a much heavier install.
3. **Decompose drives retrieval only, with at most 2 drafter calls per request** (1c).
4. **The new "fast" targets:** first token p95 ≤ 4,000 ms, total p95 ≤ 8,000 ms (1f). Also: compare the gate fallback at p95 as well as p50 (1d).
5. **Start with steps 1–2 now?** Ingestion and retrieval use no tokens and don't depend on tonight's runs.

Both runs are still in progress. I'll report as each finishes.

I've recorded both findings in the ADR and corrected [CLAUDE.md](CLAUDE.md).

**Next update:** the `qwen` budget should run out in roughly 10–15 minutes. I'll report the exact tokens-per-day figure and what the daily refusal says when it does. After that, the recovery watch runs on its own.

The five decisions from my last message are still open: who writes the evaluation questions, the embedding stack, decompose driving retrieval only, the new speed targets, and starting ingestion now. Answer whenever suits you.

### [2026-11-09 18:42] User

1) I am writing will attach next.

2) Use FastEmbed for the embedding and reranking stack.

3) Approved. Decomposition should drive retrieval only, with a maximum of two drafter calls per request.

4) Approved. Use the new p95 targets: 4,000 ms for first token and 8,000 ms for total response. Compare Option C against B at both p50 and p95.

5) Start corpus ingestion and retrieval implementation now. Keep development independent of Groq wherever possible.

i need these clarifications:
i) are the budget ledger accounts separately for the 120B drafter and 20B router/lean model. 
ii) whether extractive answers generated entirely through local code or require a 20B call, since this affects the fallback budget.

### [2026-11-09 18:42] Assistant

**i) Yes, each model has its own account.** Groq applies limits per model, and every model's headers report its own counters. Tonight's before/after check on the 20b will confirm the daily token limit is separate too. Each model gets four counters:
- requests per minute;
- tokens per minute and requests per day, both corrected from headers;
- tokens per day, which is our own estimate.

| Account | Spent on | Rules |
|---|---|---|
| `gpt-oss-120b` | Full drafts only | 20% reserve, released evenly until 18:00 |
| `gpt-oss-20b` | Router and Lean drafts | Router has first claim. Lean drafting stops below 25%. |

**ii) Extractive answers should be built entirely by local code, with no Groq call at all.** That includes deciding whether to refuse, which uses retrieval scores instead of the 20b router.
- **Why:** the fallback then costs zero tokens. It works when *both* models are out of budget, or when Groq is down. It responds in under ~500 ms.
- **Cost:** in extractive mode, an off-topic question is caught by a retrieval-score threshold rather than by the model. The threshold is weaker at catching questions the papers don't cover. We can measure how much weaker on your unanswerable questions, at zero token cost.
- **Router budget:** the 20b is then spent only when a model answer (Full or Lean) is actually possible.

I'm recording all of this, then checking the `qwen` run and starting ingestion.

**The set is solid and meets the brief.** It has 20 questions (≥15 required), including 5 the papers can't answer, plus cross-paper and number-lookup questions. I checked the three numeric questions against the parsed tables, and all are answerable:
- **Q4:** 6, 512, 2048, 8, 64/64, 100K.
- **Q9:** 1024, 4096, 16, 6, 2048, 12.5 Hz.
- **Q12:** 53.3 vs 55.5 at Large, and 55.5 vs 58.2 at XL.

Your unanswerable questions are also better than they look. Several are **near misses**, where the papers say something close by:
- **Q18:** H-Net states an 8,192-byte training context, but no inference memory limit.
- **Q20 and Q16:** Moshi names H100 training hardware, but no dollar cost and no RTX 4090 figures.
- **Q19:** Moshi discusses speakers a lot, but only two-party dialogue.

A careless system will take the bait on these, so they're a real test.

Here's what I'd change, most important first.

### 1. You need an answer key and a grading rule (blocking)
Every answer must be graded `correct`, `wrong`, `refused_correctly` or `refused_wrongly`, and reviewers will recompute those grades. Many questions are multi-part essays (Q2, Q5–Q8, Q10, Q13–Q15). Without written criteria, "correct" can't be reproduced or defended in the live conversation. For each question you need:
- the required points or values;
- the expected sections, e.g. Q9 → MOSHI Table 1, Q11 → HNET §2.2.1. These also let us measure retrieval quality with no Groq calls.

You also need **one grading rule**. I suggest a strict one: *correct = every requested part right and each claim cited to a passage that supports it. Any wrong fact or missing part = wrong.* It's harsh on long questions, but simple to defend.

### 2. Part A can be answered from memory
The brief warns: *"We will ask questions you cannot answer from memory."* Any model knows Q1–Q3 (the √d_k scaling, decoder masking, sinusoidal encodings) without retrieving anything. They'll come out "correct" even when retrieval fails, which inflates Table 3. Moshi and H-Net are recent enough that this matters less there. **I suggest replacing two of Q1–Q3** with details only the text holds. The categories in point 3 are good candidates.

### 3. Add the traps a naive retriever falls into
The write-up needs *"three ways… each with an example question"*. Ideally those examples are in this set, so their failures are *measured*:
- **Same table number in different papers. You already have this one.** Q4 cites "Table 3", Q9 "Table 1" and Q12 "Table 2", and all three papers have Tables 1–3. A keyword search on "Table 3" finds all of them. Keep these as they are.
- **A paper contradicting itself.** AIAYN gives EN-FR BLEU as 41.8 in the abstract and Table 2, but 41.0 in §6.1. No question touches this yet.
- **Facts that only appear in an appendix.** No question needs one yet. Moshi appendices A–F and H-Net appendices A–F are untested.
- **Identical captions:** Moshi Tables 3 and 17.
- **A false premise among the unanswerables.** All five ask about things the papers never discuss. A subtler one attributes something to the wrong paper. That checks whether the system refuses rather than "corrects" the premise from memory.

### 4. Q12's wording doesn't quite match the table
- The table labels the baseline **"Transformer / Token"**, not "BPE". The word "BPE" appears only elsewhere in the paper.
- "Large (760M)" and "XL (1.3B)" are named in the text and in another figure. The table itself says "FLOPs matched to GPT-3 Large/XL".

Fine as a test, but the answer key should accept that wording. One bonus finding: **H-Net (2-stage) at Large scores 55.5, the same as the Transformer at XL.** That's worth stating in the answer key.

### 5. Cost
Essay questions produce long answers, about 400–700 output tokens. 20 questions × 3 settings is roughly **60 drafts, about 220–270K tokens on the 120b**, which is more than a full day. My plan:
1. Compare retrieval settings for free first, using the expected sections, and shortlist three.
2. Spread the real answers over Nov 10 and Nov 11, within the budget ledger.

### What I need from you
1. **The grading rule:** strict as above, or something else?
2. **Would you like me to draft the answer key** (expected sections plus required points, each with its source passage) for you to check and correct? The questions stay yours; the key is mechanical work from the papers, and you'd verify every line.
3. **Will you revise Q1–Q3 and add 2–3 trap questions** (points 2 and 3), or keep the set as it is?

The Moshi numbering confirmation and your question set are both recorded in the ADR. The questions are saved at [eval/the_relay_20_evaluation_questions.txt](eval/the_relay_20_evaluation_questions.txt).

### [2026-11-10 14:54] Assistant

I've built the whole model-service layer underneath the pipeline, without a single Groq call. You've decided not to add trap questions; that's in the ADR.

## Done since you said resume

**1. Seven parser bugs fixed, and the corpus rebuilt.** The important one: dropped units such as "160 ms", because the citation check compares numbers. Also fixed:
- nested fractions, which garbled AIAYN's attention formula;
- duplicated maths symbols;
- unresolved section references;
- table debris.

A full scan finds no LaTeX left, and all 108 citations in the answer key still resolve.

**2. Stand-in calibration is generated from data.** `python -m probe.calibrate` turns the raw probe file into [standin/calibration.json](standin/calibration.json), so every number the stand-in uses can be traced and recomputed. While doing this I corrected the 503 rate to **0.64%**: 2 of ~310 calls, both on `qwen`, none on gpt-oss. That replaces the 2.5% `qwen`-only figure.

**3. Three runtime modules:**
- [relay/limits.py](relay/limits.py): budgets that refill continuously, plus Groq's 429 format. Shared by the ledger and the stand-in, so both apply the same rules.
- [relay/ledger.py](relay/ledger.py): one account per model, kept in sync from response headers and 429s, saved to disk across runs. It implements your tier policy: the 20% reserve released until 18:00, and the 25% floor on the 20b.
- [relay/llm.py](relay/llm.py): the streaming client, with a 4 s first-token limit and a total-time limit. It points at Groq or the stand-in by URL alone.

**4. The stand-in server:** [standin/server.py](standin/server.py).
- It applies Groq's limits, returns Groq-format 429s, and streams with calibrated latency, queue episodes, trickles and slow 503s.
- An admin endpoint switches scenarios: per-minute limit halved, daily budget exhausted.
- What's *assumed* rather than measured is listed in the ADR for heading 2.

**5. Smoke test passes end to end:** streaming with citations, the router's tool call, a per-minute 429 whose wait the ledger adopts, and a daily-exhausted refusal in 90 ms.

## A bug that would have corrupted results
The stand-in looked like it streamed, but it didn't: every piece of an answer arrived within 2 ms. The cause is Windows' asyncio, which treats any sleep shorter than ~15.6 ms as already due. Every stand-in "first token" would have equalled its last token, which would have quietly distorted Table 2a. It now paces against an absolute schedule: a measured 132 ms spread against ~150 ms expected.

## Next
**The pipeline itself (stages 0–6):**
- admission with the tier choice and the answer cache;
- the router tool call;
- retrieval with its loop and sub-questions;
- the decide branch;
- the streamed draft;
- the per-sentence citation gate;
- the extractive fallback;
- logging into `pipeline_rows.csv`, plus a trace file for heading 4.

All of it will be developed against the stand-in. Real Groq calls come only after that, starting with the cost-per-answer measurement, with a dry run and your OK first.

## Two things in the key will mis-grade correct answers under strict grading

**1. Q12 requires a point the reworded question no longer asks for.**
- Your new Q12 asks for the four averages, the gains, and the §3.1-versus-table discrepancy. It dropped the old "what do these results indicate" part.
- But required point 4, "Large H-Net (2-stage) matches the XL BPE Transformer", is still *required*.
- So an answer that does exactly what Q12 asks would be graded **wrong**.
- **I recommend moving point 4 to optional.**

**2. Q15's new decoder point only accepts a citation that doesn't contain it.**
- The sentence it paraphrases, "The decoder… must effectively combine coarse-grained representations from the main network with fine-grained details from the encoder residuals", is in **HNET §2.1.2**. §2.3 says the same.
- The key accepts only §2.1.1, so a correctly cited answer would fail.
- **I recommend adding HNET §2.1.2 and §2.3 as acceptable citations.**
- A small precision point as well: per §2.1.1's equation, the residual is added at the dechunking step, *before* the decoder runs. "Decoder layers combine…" is the paper's own looser phrasing in §2.1.2, so it's acceptable once that citation is allowed.

## Two small corrections to the report (records only, no effect on grading)
- It says five questions were refined, but eight changed (the three listed above).
- For Q18, the "8192 UTF-8 bytes per sequence" training setup is in **§3** (the setup text before §3.1), not §3.2. §3.2 repeats 8,192 bytes for the Chinese/code runs.

The report is your document, so I haven't edited it. Should I apply the two key fixes (Q12 point 4 to optional; §2.1.2 and §2.3 added to Q15)? After that, I'll go straight to building the pipeline.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **Groq OpenAI-compatible API and its limits.**
     - Limits measured, console-confirmed, and refilling continuously: RPM 30, TPM 8K, RPD 1K, TPD 200K.
     - TPM and TPD admission both check prompt + `max_tokens`. TPM charges the reservation; TPD charges actual usage.
     - The 429 message states an exact wait.
     - A 503 "over capacity" arrives after ~30 s.
     - Daily budgets are per model.
     - The TPD refill rate is 2.31 tokens/s.
   - **Product budget manager.**
     - One ledger account per model.
     - Tiers: Full (120b), Lean (20b), Extractive (fully local, no Groq), Cached.
     - Drafter reserve of 20%, released evenly 00:00–18:00 local.
     - Router floor of 25% on the 20b.
     - An answer cache, with hits marked `retrieval_setting=cache`.
     - Eval runs never get downgraded.
     - The L4 message gives an estimated wait, not a reset time.
   - **Pipeline design (ADR Option C).**
     - Admission → router tool call (20b) → hybrid retrieval with a loop → decide branch → streamed draft (120b) → per-sentence deterministic citation gate → emit and log.
     - Decompose drives retrieval only; at most 2 drafter calls per request.
     - Targets: first-token p95 ≤ 4000 ms, total p95 ≤ 8000 ms.
     - Option C vs B is compared at both p50 and p95.
   - **Corpus.**
     - Built from the LaTeX source, following live `\input`s only.
     - Expands macros, numbers sections, tables and figures the way LaTeX does, and resolves `\ref`.
     - Chunk sizes 128, 256 and 512 tokens (tiktoken `o200k_base`).
     - Cite labels look like "AIAYN §3.2.1" or "MOSHI Table 1".
   - **Retrieval.**
     - BM25 (rank_bm25) plus FastEmbed `BAAI/bge-small-en-v1.5`, merged with reciprocal-rank fusion (k=60).
     - Optional cross-encoder re-rank (`Xenova/ms-marco-MiniLM-L-6-v2`).
     - Re-rank costs 0.8–3.6 s of CPU; hybrid search alone takes 11–50 ms.
   - **Stand-in.**
     - An aiohttp server calibrated from `standin/calibration.json`.
     - Assumptions: load slowdown above 16 in flight, 8 s episodes, extractive content.
   - **Grading.** Strict: every required point present and cited, otherwise wrong.

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`probe/groq_probe.py`**
     - Daily phase: exhaust (every second request uses `task="ok"` with `max_tokens=600`), drain, then watch (60 s close polling, then every 15 min up to 6 h; probes again at the stated wait).
     - `--check-model`. New helpers `error_message()` and `limit_numbers()`. `build_messages(size, seed, task)`.
     - The 429 record gets `limit_limit`, `limit_used`, `limit_requested` and `stated_wait_s`.
   - **`probe/analyze_probe.py`**
     - Daily-run section: actual vs reserved tokens, fitted refill slope from the "Used" readings, first tiny and large success times, and the before/after checks.
   - **`probe/calibrate.py`** → `standin/calibration.json`, per model:
     - `queue_calm_ms` lognormal;
     - `queue_episode` share, plus `extra_ms_pooled` (mu 7.4929, sigma 0.279, n=10, median 1795 ms);
     - `rest_first_token` (log intercept 5.0316, slope 0.0002996 per prompt token, sigma 0.334, for the 120b);
     - `server_tokens_per_s` (120b 475.3, 20b 894.8);
     - trickle (120b share 0.0417, 67.3 tokens/s);
     - `rate_limited_response_ms` (median 102 ms);
     - `over_capacity_503` (share 0.0064 across all chat calls, delay 30,072 ms).
   - **`tools/build_results_csv.py`**
     - Builds `results.csv` with exactly the 12 columns from `probe_raw.jsonl` plus `results/pipeline_rows*.csv`.
     - Probe rows: `scenario=probe_*` and `retrieval_setting=none`; `answer_grade` empty; token cells empty for non-ok rows.
     - `--check` recomputes, from the CSV alone:
       - RPM: 30 ok requests, then rate-limited at 13,009 ms;
       - recovery: 2,051 ms (RPM) and 4,068 ms (TPM);
       - TPM lower bound: 5,755;
       - in-flight: 16;
       - daily: 72 ok rows, 201,298 tokens.
   - **`corpus/fetch.py`**
     - Fetches `arxiv.org/e-print/<id>v<N>` into `data/raw/<id>v<N>/`, with safe extraction and a SHA-256 manifest.
     - `PAPERS = {"AIAYN": ("1706.03762", 7, ...), "MOSHI": ("2410.00037", 1, ...), "HNET": ("2507.07955", 2, ...)}`.
   - **`corpus/latex.py`**: the parser. It provides:
     - `group`, `read_arg`, `strip_comments`, `flatten` (live `\input`s only), `collect_macros` and `expand_macros` (with arguments);
     - `\ifmmode` collapse (strip the kept branch);
     - `sub_cmd` (brace-aware `\frac`, `\sqrt`, `\SI` (siunitx units), `\si`, `\num`);
     - `clean()` (refs, footnotes, math, accents after the drop rules, the `\c` accent only as `\c{x}`, `\colorbox`, `\rule`, `\looseness`, `\multirow`/`\multicolumn` looped 3×);
     - `clean_tabular` (strips `\midrule[..]`);
     - `tokenize` (heads, floats, abstract, appendix; headings inside floats);
     - `parse` → `Block` objects. Labels placed mid-section map to the current section.
   - **`corpus/build.py`** → `data/corpus/blocks.jsonl` (433 blocks).
     - `MAIN = {"AIAYN": "ms.tex", "MOSHI": "main.tex", "HNET": "main.tex"}`.
     - `ntok` uses `disallowed_special=()`.
   - **`corpus/chunk.py`** → `chunks_{128,256,512}.jsonl`, with 800, 461 and 239 pieces.
     - Sections never cross; tables are split into row groups that repeat the caption and header.
     - `cite_of()` returns labels like "AIAYN §3.2.1" or "AIAYN Table 3".
   - **`relay/retrieval.py`**
     - `Index(size)`; `search(query, k, rerank, pool=30)` returns hits plus timing (including `top_dense_cos`).
     - Embeddings are cached in `data/index/emb_<size>_<hash>.npy`; models in `data/index/models`.
     - `get_index()` is `lru_cache`d.
   - **`relay/limits.py`**
     - `Bucket(capacity, per_s, level_at, t)` with `level`, `set_level`, `take`, `wait_for`.
     - `fmt_wait`; `error_429(model, which, limit, used, requested, wait_s)` builds Groq-format bodies; `parse_429(body)` returns `which`, `daily`, `limit`, `used`, `requested`, `wait_s`.
   - **`relay/ledger.py`**
     - `Ledger(name, limits, persist)` saves to `data/state/ledger_<name>.json`.
     - `acct`, `wait_s(model, prompt, max_tokens)`, `start`, `finish(model, usage, headers)` (charges TPD; syncs RPD and TPM from headers), `on_429` (sets the bucket so the wait equals the stated wait), `reserve_floor(when)`, `tier(full_cost, lean_cost)` returning full, lean or extractive.
     - Constants: `DRAFTER = "openai/gpt-oss-120b"`, `ROUTER = "openai/gpt-oss-20b"`, `RESERVE_SHARE = 0.20`, `RESERVE_UNTIL_HOUR = 18`, `ROUTER_FLOOR_SHARE = 0.25`.
   - **`relay/llm.py`**
     - `LLM(source="stand-in"|"real", ledger, base_url)`.
       - `REAL_URL = "https://api.groq.com/openai/v1"`, `STANDIN_URL = "http://127.0.0.1:8088/v1"`.
       - The key comes from `.env`, only when source is real.
     - `stream_chat(model, messages, max_tokens, prompt_tokens, tools, tool_choice, stream, reasoning_effort="low", first_token_s=4.0, total_s=20.0)` yields `("delta", text)`, then `("done", CallResult)`.
     - `chat(**kw)`.
     - `CallResult` fields: status (ok|rate_limited|timeout|error), `http_status`, `first_token_ms`, `total_ms`, `content`, `tool_calls`, `usage`, `headers`, `error`, `limit`, `finish_reason`.
   - **`standin/server.py`** (aiohttp; `python -m standin.server --port 8088 [--tpm-scale] [--tpd-start] [--fault-503] [--seed]`)
     - Limits are checked in the order rpd, tpd, rpm, tpm, then a Groq-format 429.
     - 503 fault; episodes; load multiplier `1 + max(0, in_flight - 16)/16`.
     - Trickle; reasoning delta, then content, then `x_groq` usage, then `[DONE]`.
     - Pacing against an absolute schedule, sleeping only when more than 20 ms ahead.
     - `extract_answer` parses `PASSAGES:` lines of the form `[CITE] text` and `QUESTION:`.
     - The router always returns a `search_papers` tool call.
     - Admin endpoints: `/admin/state`, and `/admin/config` (reset first, then `tpm_scale`, `fault_503`, `tpd_level`).
     - Constants: `CHAT_OVERHEAD = 78`, `EPISODE_S = 8`.

   - **`\c` accent regex** mangled `\citep` ("itep") and `\colorbox` ("olorbox"). Fixed by reordering and restricting the `\c` pattern.
   - **Seven parser defects:**
     - dropped siunitx units ("160 ms");
     - `=-1` debris;
     - "Section ?" references;
     - nested `\frac`;
     - `\multirow` inside `\multicolumn`;
     - `[0.3pt]` debris;
     - `\ensuremathmode` duplicating symbols and splitting paragraphs.

     All fixed and verified by a scan showing zero debris.
   - **Stand-in admin endpoint:** reset was applied after `tpd_level`, so the level was wiped. Reordered.
   - **Windows asyncio:** sleeps shorter than ~15.6 ms resolved immediately, so the stand-in streamed every piece at once (first token = last token). Fixed with absolute-schedule pacing: 132 ms measured spread vs ~150 ms expected.
   - **503 rate:** first taken as 2.5% from qwen alone; corrected to 0.64% across all chat calls.

Both are recorded in the ADR as found by me and approved by you.

## Should grading be less strict?

**I'd keep the bar on content and loosen one rule about citations.**

**Keep these strict:**
- **All required points must be present.** `answer_grade` only allows correct or wrong, so there's no partial credit to give. Any lenient line we draw would be one we chose, and reviewers can't check it. Strict is the more honest choice.
- **Refusals stay as they are.** A refusal that slips in the number is wrong.

**Loosen this:**
- Right now a citation only counts if it's on the key's list of accepted citations.
- That list can't cover every place a fact appears, which is exactly what went wrong on Q15. Papers repeat facts in the abstract, the intro and the method section.
- **Proposed change:** a citation counts if the cited passage actually supports the claim. The key's list becomes a guide, not the only allowed set.
- This doesn't make the citation requirement weaker. A citation to a passage that doesn't support the claim is still wrong.

**Add one thing that isn't a grade:**
- Record, for each answer, how many of its required points it covers (e.g. 5/6). It goes in `trace.jsonl` only.
- With only 15 answerable questions, a strict correct/wrong score may not separate the three retrieval settings well.
- Table 3 has to come from `results.csv`, so its quality column stays strict. The coverage number would only help explain it in the write-up, labelled as coming from the trace.

**Also be aware:** Q1–Q3 can be answered from memory. Strict grading keeps them honest only through the citation rule, which is one more reason to keep the citation check meaningful rather than list-based.

Should I make the citation-rule change and add point coverage to the trace? Meanwhile I'm starting on the pipeline, stages 0–6 against the stand-in. The grading rule doesn't affect that code.

### [2026-11-10 17:13] Assistant

I've applied both grading changes, and stages 0–6 of the pipeline are built and passing every test on the stand-in. No Groq budget has been spent.

## Grading changes
- **Citations now count if the cited passage supports the claim.** The key's list of citations is a guide, not the only allowed set. Uncited or wrongly cited claims are still wrong, and every required point is still needed.
- **Point coverage is recorded only in `trace.jsonl`.** It never goes into `results.csv` or changes `answer_grade`, so Table 3 stays strict.
- Both are in [eval/answer_key.json](eval/answer_key.json) and the ADR, marked as my recommendation and your approval.

## Pipeline: [relay/pipeline.py](relay/pipeline.py)
It covers each structural requirement in the brief:
- **Tool call:** the 20b router chooses between `search_papers` and `refuse_out_of_scope`.
- **Loop:** retrieval reformulates the query up to 2 more times while confidence is low.
- **Branch:** answer, refuse or decompose.
- **Retrieval step:** local hybrid search.
- **Citation gate:** checks each sentence before it is sent.

What the testing showed:
- **Questions that span two papers need one search per paper.** On the gold set, a single search for Q13 or Q15 returned only H-Net passages. Decompose now searches each paper separately.
- **The retrieval score only refuses the obvious unanswerable questions (Q16, Q19, Q20).** The near-miss ones, Q17 and Q18, score like answerable questions, so refusing those relies on the drafter. The stand-in can't refuse because it only quotes, so this can only be measured on real Groq.
- **I fixed a bug in the number check.** It let "12 layers" pass as 6 + 6. A computed number now passes only if both source numbers appear in the same sentence, e.g. "2.7 points (58.2 vs 55.5)"; Q12 needs exactly this.
- **Burst of 12 simultaneous requests on the stand-in:**
  - 4 full answers, 5 shorter answers and 3 quoted-passage answers. Nobody was turned away.
  - Most people got their first sentence in under a second; the slowest waited 16 s. The first version gave only 5 written answers, because the router calls used up the small model's per-minute budget before the shorter answers needed it.
- **With the daily budget gone (the 3pm case):**
  - People get quoted passages in about 20 ms, at 0 tokens.
  - They're told when the next full answer should be possible, about 16 minutes in this test.
- **A half-finished sentence is never sent.** If an answer is cut off, only the complete, checked sentences go out, with a note that it was cut short.

## Three decisions I need from you
I've built each one as I recommend, and they're marked pending in the ADR:
1. **Status of a downgraded answer.** I recommend `rate_limited` whenever a limit forced a weaker answer than full, including shorter or quoted-passage answers. Otherwise the daily-exhausted row in Table 2b would show 100% `ok` and hide the event.
2. **Answer tier in `retrieval_setting`.** I recommend `h256k5` for full answers, `h256k5|lean` for shorter ones, `h256k5|extractive` for quoted passages, and `cache` as already decided. The tier changes how much is retrieved, and this keeps the tier breakdown computable from `results.csv` alone.
3. **When two drafts fail the gate.** The ADR said "refuse"; I built it to show quoted passages with "I couldn't produce a written answer that checks out", which is still never wrong. Eval runs never fall back like this, so graded results aren't affected.

I also fixed citation labels for unnumbered headings: H-Net's introduction text is now "HNET §1" instead of a quoted heading title. The answer key still resolves 109/109.

**Next:** one real request to measure what a full answer actually costs. I'll show you the dry-run token cost first and wait for your OK before spending any budget.

### [2026-11-10 17:31] Assistant

I've recorded decisions 1 and 2 in the ADR as yours. Here is decision 3 explained more plainly.

## Decision 3: what the person sees when the model can't produce a checkable answer

**When it happens.** The model writes an answer, and each sentence goes through the citation check before it's sent. Sometimes nothing passes, for example:
- the model leaves out the citations;
- it cites a section we never gave it;
- it uses a number that isn't in the passage it cites.

The design allows one second try. Rarely, the second try fails too, and then we need something to show the person.

**Example:** someone asks *"How does Mimi combine semantic and acoustic information?"* and both drafts fail the check.

**Option A: refuse (what the ADR originally said)**
> Sorry, I couldn't produce an answer I can verify against the papers.

**Option B: show the relevant passages, quoted exactly (what I built)**
> I couldn't produce a written answer that checks out against the cited passages. These are the most relevant passages, quoted from the papers:
> "Mimi uses distillation to transfer non-causal, high-level semantic information into the tokens produced by a causal model…" [MOSHI §3.3.2]
> "…" [MOSHI §3.3.1]

Neither option can give a wrong answer, because Option B only quotes the papers word for word. B is more useful: the person often gets what they need, and it costs no extra tokens. This is the same quoted-passage answer people get at 3pm when the budget is gone.

It only affects real use. Eval runs never fall back like this, so graded results stay honest.

**I recommend B.** Which do you want, A or B? Once you decide, I'll run the dry run and show you the token cost before any real call.
