### [2026-11-10 17:41] Assistant

I ran five real requests: Q1, Q15 and Q16, then Q1 and Q15 again after fixes. All five are in [results/pipeline_rows_real_smoke.csv](results/pipeline_rows_real_smoke.csv), and `results.csv` rebuilds cleanly with them. Together they used about 11K tokens of the 120b's daily 200K and about 2K of the 20b's.

## Results
- **A full answer costs 1.9–2.7K tokens**, not the 3.6K I estimated. That's about **85 full answers a day** on the 120b, not 55. Reasoning used only 14–75 tokens.
- **The first sentence arrived in 1.0–2.1 s**, inside the 4 s target. Of that, Groq's own queue took 0.17–0.92 s.
- **Q16 was refused correctly:** "The papers do not say the exact power consumption…". This was the first test of the near-miss refusal path, since the stand-in can't refuse.

## Two problems the real model showed, both fixed
1. **gpt-oss writes citations in its own bracket style, `【AIAYN §3.2.1】`.** The gate read them as missing and withheld the whole first draft of Q1, which cost a second call. The bracket characters are now converted before the gate checks the label.
   - Q1 before the fix: 2 calls, 3.9K tokens, 2.1 s.
   - Q1 after the fix: 1 call, 2.2K tokens, 1.0 s.
2. **An uncited closing summary sentence was correctly withheld.** The prompt now says every sentence needs a citation, including a summary.

I also changed decompose to use the router's sub-questions first, each limited to the paper it names. That made the Moshi half of Q15 fuller.

## A retrieval trap for the write-up
H-Net's introduction lists the same stage names as its method section: encoder, chunking, main network, decoder. So with 256-token pieces, the intro (§1) outranks the actual method (§2.1.1), with or without re-ranking.
- With 512-token pieces, §2.1.1 ranks first or second, in under 1 ms and without re-ranking. Re-ranking costs 1.6–4.2 s of CPU.
- Q15's answer still cites only H-Net §1. Under strict grading it would be **wrong**, so the choice of retrieval setting matters.
- This is an example for "How this library will mislead a naive retriever".

## Next: the retrieval comparison (Table 3)
1. **Shortlist settings locally, with no Groq.** I'll score retrieval for all 20 questions against the answer key's citations across several settings, e.g. h256k5, h512k4, h512k4rr and bm256k8. For each, I'll measure how many required points are reachable from what's retrieved, the prompt tokens, and retrieval time.
2. **Run the real eval.** The best 3 settings × 20 questions comes to about 60 full answers, roughly 140K tokens on the 120b. That fits in one day's budget. I'll show you the dry-run cost and wait for your OK before starting.

Shall I start step 1?

"Points reachable" means each required point's citation is among the retrieved passages: the most a perfect drafter could answer.

| Setting | Points reachable | All points reachable | Unanswerable refused before drafting | Drafter prompt p50 (tokens) | Retrieval p50 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| bm256k8 (BM25 only, the naive baseline) | 86.6% | 11/15 | 0/5 | 2,123 | 21 ms |
| h128k8 | 79.1% | 9/15 | 0/5 | 1,347 | 126 ms |
| **h256k5 (current default)** | 73.1% | 8/15 | 0/5 | 1,432 | 66 ms |
| h256k8 | 86.6% | 10/15 | 0/5 | 2,078 | 66 ms |
| **h512k4** | 86.6% | 10/15 | 0/5 | 1,878 | 105 ms |
| h512k6 | 86.6% | 10/15 | 0/5 | 2,660 | 101 ms |
| h256k5rr | 76.1% | 9/15 | 3/5 | 1,399 | 5,119 ms |
| h512k4rr | 74.6% | 9/15 | 3/5 | 1,911 | 5,139 ms |

## What it shows
- **Re-ranking makes retrieval worse and slower.**
  - It lowers how many required points are reachable, and on Q10 it drops the one H-Net section that answers the question.
  - It takes 5–16 s per request because it runs on every search, which alone breaks the 4 s first-token target.
  - Its one strength: its score lets the pipeline refuse 3 of the 5 unanswerable questions before any drafter call, with no answerable question wrongly refused.
- **Our current default, h256k5, is the weakest hybrid.** Either bigger pieces (h512k4) or more pieces (h256k8) reaches 86.6%.
- **BM25 alone does as well as hybrid here,** because your questions use the papers' own words. That's worth stating honestly in the write-up.
- **No setting reaches some Moshi details:** the delays in Q6 and Q14, the distillation in Q7, and H-Net's problem statement in Q15. The scan uses the free heuristic router; the real LLM router's sub-questions may reach them.

## Recommended shortlist for the real eval
Each pair isolates one of the brief's three dimensions:
- **h512k4**: the likely pick. Top reachability, a mid-sized prompt, and fast.
- **h256k8**: same reachability with smaller pieces. Against h512k4 it tests piece size and pieces fetched at a similar token cost.
- **h512k4rr**: h512k4 plus re-ranking. It isolates the re-rank effect, both the early refusals and the 5 s cost.

**Cost:** 3 settings × 20 questions = 60 full answers.
- About 140K of the 120b's 200K daily tokens, and about 27K on the 20b.
- At 8K tokens per minute, the run takes about 20 minutes.
- Today's budget should be nearly full, since only about 11K was used last evening and it refills continuously.

## Decisions I need before running
1. **Do you approve the shortlist** of h512k4, h256k8 and h512k4rr?
2. **Who grades the answers?** The brief needs `answer_grade` on every eval row. I recommend:
   - I grade all 60 answers against your key, with no Groq. Each one gets a written note per required point, saved to `results/grades_eval.csv`.
   - You check a sample and overrule where you disagree. Each overrule is evidence for the "assistant got it wrong" part of the write-up.

   The alternative, an LLM judge on Groq, would spend budget and still need your check.
3. **The Option C vs B comparison** in the ADR compares checking each sentence as it streams with buffering the whole answer and judging it once. Run on the chosen setting, it would cost about 20 more answers plus 20 judge calls, roughly 60K tokens. Do you want it run tomorrow, or dropped? The brief doesn't require it; it was our own way to back up the design choice.

The answer run is 35 of 60 done after 7½ minutes. The remaining 25 will take about **5–6 more minutes**: each request waits roughly 12 s for the per-minute token budget to refill.

**Grading will take me about 30–45 minutes** once the run finishes.
- I'll read each of the 60 answers against your key and check every required point and its citation.
- Each answer gets a written note per point in `results/grades_eval.csv`.
- The 15 unanswerable-question answers are quick to grade; the cross-paper and number questions take longest.

## A problem in the timing data
The first-token times so far are **10–20 s**, against 1–2 s in yesterday's smoke test. Almost all of that is the request waiting in our own admission queue for the per-minute budget, because the runner sends the next question as soon as the last one finishes. That's my mistake in the runner: the clock starts before that wait.

- **Quality isn't affected.** The answers are valid and gradeable.
- **Table 3's first-token column would be misleading.** It would mostly measure our pacing, not the retrieval setting.

**My recommendation:**
1. Let this run finish. The answers are good for the quality and token columns.
2. Fix the runner so it waits for budget before starting the request clock. Then first-token measures the pipeline itself.
3. Tomorrow, rerun 10 questions × 3 settings (30 answers, ~75K tokens) under a separate scenario name, used only for Table 3's latency column. Both columns stay computable from `results.csv`. The Option B comparison also needs ~46K tokens tomorrow, and both fit in the day's refill.

Alternatively, rerunning all 60 tomorrow (~150K) gives clean latency plus a second quality sample. But together with Option B it would use almost the whole day's budget, with no slack for reruns.

One more early sign: **Q12 under h256k8 got no answer through the gate after two drafts.** Eval runs don't fall back to quoted passages, so that one will grade as wrong. I'll look at why when I grade it.

Should I go with the 30-answer latency rerun tomorrow?

### [2026-11-10 19:15] Assistant

The runner is fixed and the dry run passes. It now waits for budget before each request's clock starts and logs that wait separately. It also resumes only within the same scenario, so tomorrow's run starts fresh at 60 answers.

**Budget:** the 120b is at about 103K now. Since the daily budget refills continuously at 2.31 tokens/s, it's back to full around 12:30 tomorrow. A full 60-answer run fits any time after about 10:00.

## Before tomorrow: what to do with the stopped run
The stopped run left 46 real requests behind:
- their rows in `results/pipeline_rows_eval.csv`;
- their answers in `results/eval_answers.jsonl`;
- their trace events.

They also spent about 97K of the 120b's daily tokens. The project rule is to keep every raw result, because reviewers can rebuild `results.csv` and compare it against our budget numbers.

**Option A (recommended): keep it, labelled so it never counts.**

## Table 3 (real Groq, 20 questions per setting)

| Setting | Good % | Correct (answerable) | Refused correctly | Tokens/answer | First token p50 / p95 | Total p50 / p95 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **h512k4** | **45.0%** | 4/15 | 5/5 | 2,602 | 1,318 / 2,268 ms | 1,740 / 2,568 ms |
| h256k8 | 40.0% | 3/15 | 5/5 | 2,988 | 1,388 / 2,542 ms | 1,788 / 3,447 ms |
| h512k4rr | 40.0% | 3/15 | 5/5 | 2,431 | 5,583 / 14,883 ms | 5,865 / 15,229 ms |

**Refusals are solid.** All 15 unanswerable answers were refused correctly, with no guesses, including the near-misses Q17 and Q18.

**Strict correctness is low, and the notes in `results/grades_eval.csv` show why:**
- **The gate withheld correct sentences.** It's my bug, in three forms:
  - list markers "(1) (2) (3)" were checked as numbers, which cost Q5 under all three settings;
  - "100 K" with a space didn't match "100K", which cost Q4 under h512k4rr;
  - the superscript in "p^0" was read as 0, which cost Q10 under h512k4.
- **Retrieval missed passages.** Two of these are naive-retriever traps for the write-up:
  - The router's rewritten query missed AIAYN Table 3, so Q4 was **refused wrongly** under two settings. h256k8 pulled H-Net's Table 1 instead, the "every paper has a Table N" trap.
  - H-Net's Table 2 is split into row chunks, and that confused Q12 under every setting.
- **The drafter left out detail or got facts wrong.** For example, it said "τ = 2 is a 2-second delay" when 2 steps is 160 ms.

## What I need from you before step 3
1. **Which setting?** I recommend **h512k4**. It has the best quality, the fewest tokens among the two fast settings, and a first-token p95 of 2.3 s against our 4 s target. Re-ranking misses that target badly (14.9 s) and doesn't improve quality.
2. **Should I fix the three gate bugs before step 3?** I recommend yes. They're small, testable fixes; Table 3 stays as measured, with the cause noted. Option B doesn't use the gate, so the comparison stays fair either way.
3. **How should I grade the five borderline points?** Read strictly, they require details the question never asks for:
   - Q3: the wavelength progression
   - Q7: the codec numbers
   - Q10: that the main network can itself be an H-Net
   - Q11: that the probabilities are passed to dechunking
   - Q13: that only chunking changes the sequence length

   Strict keeps the table above. Lenient raises correct answers to 5/15 for h512k4, 7/15 for h256k8 and 6/15 for h512k4rr, which would change the ranking. I'd keep strict, because that's the rule you set, but it's your rubric.

## Step 3 cost and timing
Option B on h512k4 costs about 49K tokens on the 120b, and its judge about 60K on the 20b. The 120b has 71K right now, which is under the 15% slack line. It will have enough by about 15:00 today, with time to refill fully before tomorrow evening's real load runs.

## 2) Gate fixed
The three false positives are gone: list markers "(1) (2) (3)", "100 K" with a space, and "p^0"-style notation. I replayed the fixed gate on every sentence it had withheld in the real eval, and all six wrongly withheld sentences now pass. Made-up numbers like "300 K steps" are still caught.

The replay also showed a blind spot. The fix lets through three *wrong* Q12 sentences, like "53.3% at both scales", which had been caught only by accident. The number really is in the passage but is attached to the wrong row, and the gate can't see that. It's a real example for the write-up.

## 3) Grading made less strict
**The rule now:** a required point counts if its main idea is right; specific numbers, names or formulas are needed only if the question asks for them. Wrong facts and citations that don't support their claim still fail. Each answer whose grade changed keeps its old strict grade in its note.

| Setting | Good % | Correct (of 15 answerable) | First token p50 / p95 | Tokens/answer |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| h512k4 | 50.0% | 5 | 1,318 / 2,268 ms | 2,602 |
| **h256k8** | **65.0%** | **8** | 1,388 / 2,542 ms | 2,988 |
| h512k4rr | 60.0% | 7 | 5,583 / 14,883 ms | 2,431 |

## 1) Your setting choice: please confirm
You chose h512k4 because I recommended it from the strict grades. Under the new rule, **h256k8 is clearly better**:
- it gets 3 more correct answers;
- none of its answers states a wrong fact that slipped past the gate, against 4 of 14 for h512k4;
- the costs are ~15% more tokens and about 0.3 s more at the slow end of first-token times.

Both stay well within the 4 s first-token target. **I now recommend switching to h256k8.** Keep h512k4, or switch?

## Step 3: C vs B, with me as the judge, at 0 tokens
I removed the Groq-judge code without ever running it. In Option B nothing is sent until the whole draft is finished. So B's first token is simply C's finish time for the same request, and both times are already in `results.csv`. My grading serves as the judge.

| Setting | C first token p50 | B first token p50 | C faster by | Wrong facts that passed the gate |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| h512k4 | 1,318 ms | 1,740 ms | 24.3% | 28.6% |
| h256k8 | 1,388 ms | 1,788 ms | 22.3% | 0.0% |

Checked against the switch-to-B rules we set before having any data:
- **Speed:** C is 22–24% faster than a B with no judge, short of the 30% bar. But a real B adds a judge call, and the bar is met once that call takes more than about 0.14 s. No Groq call we've measured has been that fast.
- **Gate-fail rate:** 9–14% of sentences, under the 25% limit.
- **Wrong facts that pass the gate:** this rule (more than 10%) is broken at h512k4 but not at h256k8. That's another reason for h256k8.

So C stands, provided we use h256k8. Once you confirm the setting, I'll record it, update the pipeline default, and move on to the load runs.

### [2026-11-11 07:58] Assistant

I re-read all four pages of the PDF and checked each requirement against what's built and measured. Below is where we stand, starting with what reviewers will check first.

## Already solid
- **Pipeline structure:** 7 steps, a tool call (the 20b router choosing `search_papers` or `refuse_out_of_scope`), a loop (retrieval rewrites the query and retries), a branch (answer / refuse / decompose), and a retrieval step.
- **Library and citations:** papers parsed from the LaTeX source at the exact versions. Every sentence cites a section, table or figure, and the gate enforces it.
- **Refusals:** 15/15 unanswerable questions refused correctly, including the near-misses Q17 and Q18.
- **Real probes:** every limit was measured, and the console confirms the values. The daily refill rate was measured directly.
- **Stand-in:** it can be slow, fail, refuse at a limit, slow down under load and stream, all calibrated from a file the probes produce.
- **Table 3:** three settings, with one chosen and the reason recorded.
- **`results.csv`:** has exactly the 12 required columns and is rebuilt only from raw logs.

## A. Gaps that would make the submission incomplete
1. **Table 2a/2b doesn't exist yet.** It's the most heavily weighted part ("reviewers read these first"), and all 5 load scenarios are still to run.
2. **Two Table 2b columns aren't directly computable from `results.csv`:**
   - **Peak queue length:** the CSV has no queue column. Fix: define it as "peak requests in the system at once", computed from `start_time` and `total_ms` overlaps, and say so.
   - **Cost per 1,000 answers:** `tokens_in`/`tokens_out` mix router tokens (20b price) with drafter tokens (120b price), and the CSV doesn't say which model. Fix: price the drafter-tier tokens by the `|lean` suffix and the router share by its measured average, and state that assumption. Otherwise the reviewers' recomputation won't match ours.
3. **Table 3's columns don't match the brief yet.** The brief asks for "Correct and cited %", "Wrong %" and "Correctly refused %", all out of the 20 questions. We need to decide where `refused_wrongly` goes; I'd put it under "Wrong" so the three columns add up to 100%.
4. **Heading 6 needs a real case where retrying made things worse.** So far nothing has: our real runs had 0 retries. The stand-in experiment might not show it either, and if it doesn't we report that honestly.
5. **Heading 4 needs one slow request** split into waiting, working and retrying. Tomorrow's real burst will give us good candidates, since its trace records admission waits and Groq's own queue time.

## B. Credibility risks (the brief puts the most weight here)
The brief says: *"what we are looking at is how well you direct it… where you overrule it… choices that follow from your measurements."* Reviewers will read the raw conversation history.

6. **We relaxed the grading rule after seeing the results, and that flipped the setting choice.** The history shows the reason given: "the results make it seem as if we have a useless RAG system". A reviewer could read that as moving the goalposts.
   - **Mitigation:** report both gradings in the write-up (strict 45/40/40%, less strict 50/65/60%). Base the rule change on its principle: details the question never asks for shouldn't be required.
   - **I recommend you spot-check the grades yourself.** It was part of the plan we agreed and hasn't happened. Right now every grade is mine.
7. **We deleted a real run's raw results.** The brief says *"Keep the raw results… We will look at them"*. The deletion is visible in the conversation history, and about 97K tokens of real spend have no rows. One honest line in the write-up would turn this from looking hidden into an engineering call.
8. **Table 3 measured the old gate.** The three gate fixes came after the eval, so the shipped system differs from what Table 3 measured. Say so in one line, or rerun h256k8 (about 60K tokens).
9. **Q1–Q3 can be answered from memory,** as we noted earlier. They pass on grounding, not retrieval.
10. **Using a third model (qwen) to burn a whole daily budget, and splitting work across two models' quotas.** Both are within the rules, since limits are per model, but the brief warns against "any trick to get around its limits". Frame it plainly: qwen was used only for measurement, and the 20b/120b split is by job, not to get more capacity.

## C. Weak points in the system itself
11. **The router's rewritten query can make retrieval worse.** It caused Q4's two wrong refusals: the rewrite never retrieved AIAYN Table 3, and confidence was high enough that the loop never retried. Fix: always also search the original question and merge the results (about 20 ms).
12. **Tables split into row chunks confuse the model,** which caused Q12's wrong answers under every setting. Fix: keep a table whole up to about 600 tokens.
13. **The gate can't catch a real number attached to the wrong row** (Q12 "53.3% at both scales"). That gap is known and accepted, but it's the gate's biggest weakness, so say it in heading 1.
14. **Retrieval has no timeout.** The brief says "Tools time out". The router call has one, and retrieval doesn't. It's 66 ms now, but adding a deadline costs nothing.
15. **The per-sentence gate's speed advantage is smaller than planned:** 22–24% faster at the median, against the 30% bar, because many answers are one or two long sentences. The defence holds (any real judge call would cost more than the 143 ms gap), but a reviewer may push on it.
16. **The 3pm answer may not be what heading 7 describes.** When the 120b's daily budget runs out, the ledger switches to *shorter answers from the 20b*, and quotes passages only once the 20b also drops below its 25% floor. The write-up and the "daily budget exhausted" row must describe that two-step behaviour, or the run will contradict the text.
17. **How long people wait is our least certain assumption.** The brief says people "give up sooner than you expect", but we have no data. Patience drives everything about shedding to quoted passages, so it's the natural answer for heading 8.

## D. Schedule and budget risks
18. **Deadline:** "within 4 days of receiving this brief". If you received it on Nov 9, the deadline is **Nov 13**. Please confirm, because it fixes how much buffer we have.
19. **Running the real daily-exhaustion test on Nov 12 evening leaves no 120b budget for reruns before submission.** It refills about 24 hours later.
20. **Two pages is very tight.** 8 headings, 3 extra sections, 4 tables and the assistant section won't fit comfortably. Start drafting early, so we know what has to be cut.
21. **The live session** will ask you to "explain your results and change your system". `relay/pipeline.py` is about 700 lines. A walkthrough before submitting would help.

## What I'd do, in order
1. **Today, no Groq:**
   - Fix 11, 12 and 14.
   - Build the load generator, with Table 2's definitions for peak queue and cost written into `tools/tables.py`.
   - Run sustained overload, per-minute-limit-halved and the retry experiment on the stand-in.
   - Settle the Table 3 columns.
2. **You:** spot-check about 10 grades, and confirm the deadline.
3. **Tomorrow evening, real Groq:**
   - Steady, then burst, then continue until the 120b's daily budget is gone. Define "exhausted" as the 120b, with the 20b's shorter answers and then quoted passages visible in the rows.
   - Pick the heading-4 request from this run.
4. **Write-up draft:** start tomorrow, in parallel.

**Done today:**
- **Quality fixes:**
  - the original question is always searched alongside the router's rewrite;
  - a table the question names is looked up directly;
  - whole tables are given to the drafter;
  - paper detection matches whole words;
  - the retrieval tool has a timeout with a fallback;
  - the drafter prompt has tighter rules.

  The local check shows no regression.
- **Table 2 definitions**, as you approved:
  - Peak queue = the most requests in the system at once.
  - Cost = priced by tier, as an upper bound.

### [2026-11-11 09:08] Assistant

Today's stand-in work is done and committed (`f9e97fb`). Heading 6 has its measured case, and three of the five Table 2a/2b rows are filled.

## Table 2a/2b so far (stand-in rows)
| Scenario | Requests | First token p50 / p95 | Full answer | Weaker answer (rate-limited) | Gave up |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Sustained overload (22.8/min) | 113 | 69 / 19,910 ms | 15.0% | 84.1% | 0.9% |
| Per-minute limit halved | 27 | 582 / 11,344 ms | 25.9% | 74.1% | 0.0% |
| Daily budget exhausted | 19 | 915 / 17,504 ms | 0.0% | 100.0% | 0.0% |

The pattern for heading 5:
- **Under overload, people get quoted passages within about 70 ms** instead of queueing, so almost nobody gives up.
- **With the 120b's daily budget gone,** 11 of 19 got shorter answers from the 20b and 8 got quoted passages. That two-step fallback is what heading 7 needs to describe.

## Heading 6: retrying made things worse
Naive immediate retries made the median first token more than twice as long (2.2 s to 4.7 s). They also reserved 81% more per-minute budget and caused 10 rate-limit rejections, all for one extra full answer.

The first attempt at the experiment is kept as well, and it's a finding too: under a burst, our admission control turns people away before any model call, so retries almost never happen.

## Remaining
- **Tonight: the Table 3 rerun on the fixed pipeline.**
  - It's 60 real answers, about 150K of the 120b's tokens, recorded as a new scenario `eval_v2`, so the first run's rows stay.

  - **OK to run it at ~22:30?** I'll dry-run first.
- **Tomorrow:**
  - Grade the 60 answers, morning.
  - Real steady, then burst, continuing until the daily budget is gone, evening.
  - Heading 4 from the slowest request in that run.
  - The write-up draft.
- **Still open from the review:**
  - Your spot-check of about 10 grades.

### [2026-11-11 11:14] Assistant

It looks bad on the surface, but the headline number undersells it. Three things to know before judging it.

**1. 50% is out of a 75% ceiling.** The brief's column divides by all 20 questions. Five of them are designed to be refused, and those can never count as "correct and cited". So the most any system could score in that column is 75%.
- On the 15 answerable questions, h256k8 got **10 of 15 (66.7%)**.
- It refused all 5 unanswerable ones correctly, so **15 of 20 outcomes are right (75.0%)**.

**2. The failures are almost all "missing", not "false".** That's the failure the brief cares about most: "never send a wrong or embarrassing answer". Here are h256k8's five misses:

| Q | What went wrong | Cause | Fixed tonight? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q4 | Refused a question the papers answer | Retrieval missed AIAYN Table 3 | Yes: the original question is always searched, and named tables are looked up |
| Q5 | The three limitations were withheld | Gate bug: "(1) (2) (3)" read as numbers | Yes: gate fixed |
| Q9 | Said the papers don't give 3 of 6 values | Retrieval missed MOSHI Table 1 | Yes: named-table lookup |
| Q12 | Incomplete table comparison | Table split into fragments | Yes: whole tables |
| Q15 | Thin on both papers' motivations | A hard cross-paper question; the drafter left things out | Partly: the prompt asks for every part |

**h256k8 made zero misreads that slipped past the gate.** None of its sent answers stated something false about a paper. Q9 is the one partial exception: "the papers do not provide" is itself untrue. The worst failure was saying too little, never making something up.

**3. The rubric is deliberately hard.** Every explicitly asked component must be present, every citation must support its claim, and these are multi-part questions written so they can't be answered from memory. Under a looser "mostly right" standard, most of the misses would pass.

**The honest caveat:** four of the five causes are fixed, but the fixes were made on these same 20 questions. So tonight's `eval_v2` score will flatter us somewhat. If it lands at 13–14 of 15, some of that gain is real and some is fitting to the test.

**What I'd do:**
- **Wait for `eval_v2`** before judging. It's the system we're actually shipping.
- **Report three numbers in the write-up,** so a reviewer doesn't read 50% as half-broken: the brief's column (out of 20), correct on answerable questions (out of 15), and "stated something false" (0 for h256k8).
- **If you have 20 minutes tomorrow, write 5 new questions** I've never seen and that no fix was tuned on, and we run them once (about 15K tokens). That's the honest measure of whether the fixes generalise, and it would answer the obvious reviewer question.

i have a few strategies which might/might not make our system better. Please go through them, search about them and lmk if it'll help improve our workflow.
1) prefix caching: if we are processing the same system prompt for every user.
2) CQRS - split your brain: used as ignestion and querying have completely different needs and applicable if our system is forcing them through the same pipeline.
3) HyDE retrieval: embeds the answer, not the question. Cost: one extra LLM call per query. apparently claimed that retrieval accuracy jumps drastically and that it's worth it for complex queries.
4) ColBERT - late interaction: one vector per document misses everything. token level matching changes the game. it's basically: does this specific sentence answer this specific question. Tradeoff: more storage but better retrieval.
go through this and lmk if it makes sense to implement any of these ideas.

### [2026-11-11 11:28] Assistant

I've checked all four against current sources and against our own measurements. Two are worth doing in small form; two I'd leave out on purpose.

## 1. Prefix caching: already happening; worth two small changes
- **What Groq does:** prompt caching is automatic and supported only on gpt-oss-20b, gpt-oss-120b and gpt-oss-safeguard-20b. A hit needs an exact match at the start of the prompt, and the minimum cacheable length is 128–1,024 tokens depending on the model.
- **The payoff:** cached input is 50% cheaper and, per Groq, doesn't count toward rate limits, though it's still subtracted after processing.
- **We already get some.** I checked our real eval calls: **7,680 of 117,962 drafter prompt tokens were cached (6.5%)**. Our prompt is already in the right order: fixed instructions first, then passages, then the question last.
- **There's little more to gain.** After the shared system prompt, every prompt differs because the passages differ. The cases where the whole prompt repeats, an identical question, are already served by our answer cache with no model call at all.
- **Two cheap changes** (about 15 minutes, no tokens):
  - record `cached_tokens` per request in the trace;
  - have the budget ledger credit cached tokens back, since Groq doesn't count them against limits.

  The 6.5% also makes a nice "number nobody could guess" candidate.

## 2. CQRS (separate ingestion from querying): not applicable
Our system is already split. Ingestion runs offline once: fetch the LaTeX, parse, chunk, build the index. The query path only reads a fixed index. Nothing is forced through a shared pipeline, so there's nothing to separate.

## 3. HyDE (search with a drafted hypothetical answer): I'd not build it
- **It's riskiest exactly where we're strongest.** HyDE's hypothetical answer can contain made-up details, and the risk is highest in narrow, fact-bound domains. One critique calls the effect "knowledge leakage": the model recalls memorised facts instead.
- **That would hit our refusals.** For Q16 ("RTX 4090 power draw"), the model would invent a plausible number and pull hardware passages toward it, making the drafter likelier to answer something it should refuse. We're 15/15 on refusals today.
- **Our misses weren't vocabulary gaps.** They came from tables split into pieces, table numbers that exist in every paper, and the gate bugs. HyDE fixes none of those.
- **The cost is small, but the gain is unclear.** It could ride inside the existing router call, adding about 100 output tokens on the 20b, so there'd be no extra request.

This makes a good "deliberately didn't build" item for heading 8, with that reasoning.

## 4. ColBERT (token-level late interaction): worth a quick test with zero Groq tokens
- **It fits our failed re-rank slot.** The re-ranker we tried hurt quality and cost 1.6–4.2 s. A ColBERT re-ranker scores each candidate passage token by token against the question, and could fix the "H-Net introduction outranks the method section" trap.
- **Runs locally.** FastEmbed (our existing library) supports `answerdotai/answerai-colbert-small-v1`: about 130 MB, 96-dimensional. Its authors report it beating the original ColBERTv2.
- **Storage is trivial at our size:** 461 pieces × ~210 tokens × 96 dimensions comes to about 37 MB.
- **Qdrant's guidance matches how we'd use it:** re-rank a small retrieved set rather than search the whole corpus.
- **The plan:**
  - add ColBERT re-ranking as a setting (`h256k8cb`) and run our existing local scan, with no Groq;
  - adopt it only if it beats h256k8 on reachable points while staying fast.

  It's about an hour. If it wins, it needs 20 real answers (about 60K tokens) to enter Table 3, and tomorrow's budget is already committed to the load runs. So realistically it gives either a measured local result for the write-up, or a short real check if the budget allows.

## My recommendation
1. **Do 1 now:** the cached-token logging and the ledger credit.
2. **Run the 4 experiment locally today.**
3. **Skip 2 and 3,** and name HyDE in heading 8 as deliberately not built, with the reason.

None of this touches tonight's `eval_v2` run, which uses the system as it stands.

Shall I go ahead with 1 and the ColBERT test?

- [Groq blog: gpt-oss prompt caching and lower pricing](https://groq.com/blog/gpt-oss-improvements-prompt-caching-and-lower-pricing)
- [Groq: rate limits](https://console.groq.com/docs/rate-limits.md)

### [2026-11-11 12:23] Assistant

No. The brief doesn't ask for a UI, and I'd recommend against building one.

**What the brief asks for:** "a small working version of the pipeline in Python", the results tables, `results.csv`, a 2-page write-up and the conversation history. Heading 7 asks you to *describe* the reply a person receives, and we already have the exact text, captured from the pipeline in `results/reply_at_3pm_2026-11-11.txt`.

**Why I wouldn't build one:**
- **It isn't graded.** The brief says what they're looking for: "how well you direct [the assistant]… the choices, measurements and reasoning". A UI adds none of that.
- **Time is short.** The deadline is Nov 13, tomorrow evening goes to the real runs, and the write-up still needs your sections and a cut to 2 pages.
- **It's more to defend live.** The follow-up session asks you to "explain your results and change your system". Extra front-end code is surface area with no payoff.

**For the live session, the command line already does the job.** `python -m relay.pipeline "question"` streams each sentence as it passes the citation gate, and shows notices such as "Shorter answer…" in brackets, so you can show behaviour live. A few prepared commands would cover a demo:
- one normal question;
- one unanswerable question (refused);
- the 3pm script (`python -m eval.show_3pm`);
- a stand-in load run.

If you'd like, I can write those into a short `DEMO.md` cheat sheet tomorrow. It's about 10 minutes and no tokens.

### [2026-11-12 08:13] User

which one do you suggest??

### [2026-11-12 08:13] Assistant

I recommend the plan as proposed: **steady and burst now, the drain at 18:05 on a timer.** The alternative is to run all three tonight, after 18:00, as originally planned.

**Why now is better:**
- **Room to recover.** If steady or burst goes wrong, say a bug, a Groq outage or a bad row, there's still budget and time today to fix it and rerun. If all three run tonight and one fails, the 120b is already drained, and the rerun lands tomorrow, the deadline day.

- **The drain can't start earlier anyway.** The daily reserve isn't fully released until 18:00.

**The one cost:** steady and burst will come from the afternoon on Groq and the drain from the evening. On Day 1 we saw slightly different slow-end behaviour between those windows (afternoon trickles, evening queue spikes). Table 2a gives each row its own times, so this just needs one sentence in the write-up, and it shows both kinds of slow request.

     - Then: "let's do steady and burst now, and then drain at 18:05."

2. **Key Technical Concepts**
   - **Groq limits** (console-confirmed and rechecked on Nov 12):
     - RPM 30, TPM 8K, RPD 1K, TPD 200K per model;
     - continuous refill at 2.31 tokens/s;
     - `max_tokens` is reserved against TPM up front;
     - prefix caching on gpt-oss: cached tokens cost 50% less and don't count toward limits; 6.5% of eval prompt tokens were cached.
   - **Pipeline (Option C):**
     - admission: answer cache, ledger tiers (Full 120b / Lean 20b / Extractive / cached), FIFO admission per model, shedding when the estimated wait exceeds patience minus a 2 s margin;
     - L1 counts `pending_full`;
     - the router (gpt-oss-20b tool call) steps aside when 20b TPM headroom is below 30%;
     - retrieval loop: the router query and the original question are searched together and merged round-robin;
     - named-table lookup; whole tables given to the drafter (≤1,200 tokens);
     - retrieval timeout 2 s (12 s with re-rank), falling back to BM25 only;
     - decide branch: answer / refuse / decompose;
     - streamed draft through a per-sentence deterministic gate; at most 2 drafter calls; the cut-off tail is dropped;
     - retry policy plus a 10% retry budget.
   - **Grading:** a two-level rubric frozen in `answer_key.json` (commit c64ccf8).
     - Level 1 (must pass) decides correct/wrong. Level 2 is partial credit only.
     - Level 2 never rescues a material factual error.
     - A citation counts if the cited passage supports the claim.
   - **Settings:** default **h256k8** (author's decision); setting regex `(h|bm)(\d+)k(\d+)(rr|cb)?`.
   - **Table 2 definitions** (approved by the author):
     - peak queue = most requests in the system at once, from `start_time`/`total_ms` overlaps;
     - cost = an upper bound priced by tier (Full rows at 120b prices, other tiers at 20b prices);
     - in Table 3, "Wrong" includes `refused_wrongly`.
   - **Load generator:**
     - Poisson arrivals;
     - 25% cache-eligible repeats; the rest bypass the cache via `use_cache=False`;
     - lognormal patience, median 12 s;
     - give-ups cancel the request and are logged as timeout.

3. **Files and Code Sections**
   - **`relay/pipeline.py`** (Relay class):
     - `ask(question, *, request_id, patience_s, on_event, use_cache=True, scenario=None)`;
     - Req fields: `use_cache`, `cached_in`, `truncated`, `on_event`;
     - methods: `search` (with timeout and BM25 fallback, `late=self.setting.late`), `merge`, `retrieve_loop`, `plan_parts`, `decompose`, `named_tables`, `expand_tables`, `cap_passages`, `draft`, `call_drafter`, `should_retry`, `gate`, `extractive`, `admit`, `l1(full_cost)`, `daily_gone`, `est_prompt`, `emit`, `count`, `_finish`, `result`;

     - `check_citations` and `SentenceSplitter`.

| Real run | Full (120b) | **Shorter answer (20b)** | Quoted passages (no model) | From the cache |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Steady (27) | 24 | **2** | 1 | 0 |

| Drain (100) | 73 | **13** | 0 | 14 |
| Daily budget exhausted (20) | 0 | **18** | 0 | 2 |
| **Total (167)** | 98 | **37** | 11 | 21 |

There were two reasons for a downgrade:

1. **Per-minute pressure** (steady, burst, and the 13 during the drain). The 120b's per-minute budget was busy, and the estimated wait for it was longer than that person's patience. They got the 20b at once instead of waiting. In the burst, the 20b's own per-minute budget ran out too, so 10 people got quoted passages within about 50 ms.
2. **The daily budget** (the 18 at the end). After the drain, the 120b's daily level stayed at about 1.9–2.3K tokens. It kept refilling, but never reached the ~2.8K a full answer needs. So every request went to the 20b, with the "Shorter answer: today's budget for full answers is used up…" notice.

In Table 2b, the "Rate-limited %" column counts the 20b answers and the quoted-passage answers together. That's why burst shows 70.0%: 4 + 10 out of 20.

### [2026-11-12 12:57] User

right now you're grading it, but what can be used as grading in production??

### [2026-11-12 12:58] Assistant

In production there's no answer key, and nobody grades every answer. Quality is tracked through a few layers, from cheapest to most expensive. The first already exists in our pipeline.

**1. Free signals on every request (already in our trace)**
- **Gate withholding rate:** the share of drafted sentences the citation gate blocked. A rising rate means the drafter is making claims the passages don't support. For example, 6 of the 18 shorter 20b answers in the daily-exhausted run had a sentence withheld.
- **Refusal rate, split by retrieval confidence:** a refusal when retrieval confidence was high is suspicious, because the papers probably did cover the question.
- **Tier mix and truncation:** how often people got a 20b answer, quoted passages, or an answer cut off.

These cost nothing, but they're proxies. They catch unsupported claims, not a correct number taken from the wrong table row.

**2. What the user does**
- Thumbs up or down on an answer.
- Rephrasing the same question within a minute, which is a strong sign the answer missed.
- Copying the answer or clicking a citation, which are weak signs it helped.

**3. A model grading a small sample, offline**
- Grade 2–5% of answers without an answer key:
  - Does each claim follow from the passage it cites? This is the semantic version of our gate.
  - Does the answer address the question?
  - Was a refusal justified by what was retrieved?
- Run it after hours on daily budget that would otherwise go unused. The budget refills continuously, so anything unspent by the evening is free. This is the per-request Groq judge you rejected, but as a small sample it fits the budget.

**4. The gold set as a regression check**
- Re-run your 20 questions against the frozen key whenever the prompt, model or retrieval setting changes, and weekly to catch Groq changing the model underneath us.
- That's the only place the four grades (correct / wrong / refused_correctly / refused_wrongly) exist in production.
- Each run costs about 60K tokens, 30% of a day, so run it at night or on a subset.

**5. People review the risky cases**
- A person reads a targeted sample, not a random one: answers where the gate withheld something, high-confidence refusals, shorter 20b answers, and answers users rephrased.

**Something we can do now for no tokens:** check whether the layer 1 signals actually predict the 80 grades we already have. For example, are answers with a withheld sentence or low retrieval confidence more often wrong? If they are, we can say in the write-up that we know which free signal to watch in production, and that we've measured how well it works.

I can run that check, or grade the 172 real load answers as I suggested last turn, or both. Either way, a sentence about this would fit under heading 8 as something we left out.

**1. Grading the 172 real load answers.** All 172 are graded against your frozen rubric, with the same precedents as eval_v2. Answers that only quote passages count as refusals, as you agreed.

The daily request limit (RPD) is now measured: **1,000 per day**. In Table 1 it's raised from medium to high confidence, and the count can be recomputed from `results.csv`. Committed as `9f79464`.

- **What I ran:** about 2,000-token's worth of tiny requests, one every 2.2 s, on qwen (the pipeline doesn't use it). After **exactly 1,000 successful requests in 38.3 minutes**, Groq returned a 429 saying "requests per day (RPD): Limit 1000, Used 1000". One request got through again 90 s later.
- **Cost:** 51K of qwen's tokens and nothing from the 120b or 20b.

**An unexpected finding:** the request budget did not refill at all while requests kept arriving.
- A steady refill of one request per 86.4 s would have allowed about 26 more during the run. There were none.
- Groq's reset header grew by exactly 86.4 s with every request and never shrank as time passed.
- Yet after 90 s of no traffic, one request was allowed again.
- So the budget seems to refill only during gaps of 86.4 s or more. Under steady traffic it's a hard 1,000 per day.
- **It doesn't affect our system:** the daily token budget runs out first, at about 70 full answers a day.
- It's a good candidate for your heading 8 "something new I learned" or for "the number nobody could guess". You'd only know it by running out on purpose.

**Rebuilt `results.csv`:** 2,015 rows, now including the 172 graded load answers and the RPD rows. I recomputed Tables 2 and 3 from it, and they're unchanged. Every Table 1 figure except two comes from the CSV.

**Write-up:** the RPD row and the recovery row ("90 s (RPD)") are updated, and it still fits 2 pages with about 300 words for your sections.

**Two decisions still need you:**
1. **How Table 1 shows TPM 8,000 and TPD 200,000.** The CSV can only show "≥5,755 tokens per minute" and "201,298 tokens before the daily 429". The exact limits come from Groq's 429 messages and the console. Option A: label those two cells "Groq states". Option B: add a footnote and keep the current wording.
2. **Optional:** spot-check some of the 172 load grades, as I suggested in my last message.

Keep the RPD result at 1,000/day with high confidence. Include the continuous-traffic versus 90-second idle recovery observation as a key experimental finding. 2. **Key Technical Concepts**
   - **Groq limits** (per model): RPM 30, TPM 8K, RPD 1,000, TPD 200K.
     - TPD refill is ~2.31 tokens/s, measured while idle.
     - `max_tokens` is reserved against TPM up front.
   - **New RPD finding** (stated as an inference):
     - With a request every ~2.2 s, no request was credited back over the 38.3-minute run. A steady 1-per-86.4 s refill would have allowed ~26 more.
     - The `x-ratelimit-reset-requests` header grew by exactly 86.4 s per request.
     - After a 90 s idle gap, one request was accepted.
   - **Answer tiers:** Full (120b), Lean (20b), Extractive (quoted passages, no model call), Cached.
     - 20% reserve, released evenly until 18:00.
   - **Grading:** two-level rubric frozen in `eval/answer_key.json`.
     - Extractive answers count as refusals (author's rule): `refused_correctly` for unanswerable questions, `refused_wrongly` for answerable ones.
     - Cache hits inherit the grade of the answer they replay.

### [2026-11-12 18:25] User

Three prompts had the biggest impact on the project. First, I asked it to recheck the entire assignment brief against the implementation and identify anything remaining. This exposed gaps in per-day degradation handling, the report's page limit and submission requirements. Second, I asked, "Right now you're grading it, but what can be used as grading in production?" This shifted the discussion beyond offline evaluation towards practical monitoring signals such as citation-gate failures, refusal rates, user feedback and sampled quality reviews. Third, I asked it to grade all 172 real load answers and investigate Groq's daily request limit using Qwen. This helped quantify the quality trade-off between the 120B and 20B models under actual load and produced an unexpected finding about quota recovery.

One issue I caught was that 172 real user-facing load responses had been left ungraded in `results.csv`, even though they came from our known evaluation questions and could be checked against the answer key. I questioned the empty `answer_grade` fields and identified this gap. We subsequently graded those responses, allowing us to measure the actual quality difference between the full and lean answer tiers.

I also chose to run the 1,000-request daily-limit experiment on Qwen despite the initial recommendation to preserve its quota. Since development was nearly complete and the pipeline did not use Qwen, I felt the unused budget was better spent obtaining stronger experimental evidence. The experiment confirmed the 1,000 RPD limit and revealed unexpected recovery behaviour under continuous traffic.

The engineering decisions and validation were mine: the 20% reserve, sentence-level citation gate, fallback priorities, evaluation criteria and manual verification. I also reviewed the evaluation questions against the papers and independently checked the grading. The assistant helped implement and analyse the system, but I made the final trade-offs and checked its conclusions against the evidence.

## Verdict: strong submission, with one real gap

**What passes, checked in the clone rather than taken on trust:**
- **Raw file:** `results.csv` has exactly the brief's 12 columns and 2,015 rows. Rebuilding it from the raw logs gives an identical file.
- **Tables:** all four recompute from `results.csv` and match the write-up figure for figure: Table 1, all 5 rows of 2a/2b, and Table 3 including the shipped-pipeline row.
- **Tests:** the gate tests pass, the answer-key check verifies 20/20 (109 cites, none unknown), and every pipeline path in the smoke test passes (burst, TPM halved, daily gone, give-up).
- **Write-up:** every heading is there in the brief's order, including the extra headings in the right places. It's 2 pages. The number nobody could guess, the 15+ self-written questions, tokens per answer, the waiting/working/retrying split, and a case where retrying made things worse are all present.
- **Ground rules:**
  - The key is in no file and no commit.
  - The papers are at their exact versions.
  - One account.
  - Submitted within 4 days (the brief arrived Nov 9).

## What a reviewer would flag

**1. The conversation history is partial.** This is the only real gap. The brief asks for "your real conversation history… not a cleaned-up one". `agent-convo-history.txt` starts on the afternoon of Nov 12 ("Steady and burst are done…") and holds 13 prompts. It misses Nov 9–11: the probes, the design, the budget plan, the eval. Two of the moments your section relies on aren't in it: "let q1-3 be as it is" and "you be the judge". A reviewer can't verify those claims from the file.
   - **Fix:** add the two raw session files alongside it. I can copy them into a `conversation/` folder with your email replaced by `[redacted]`, scan them for the key, and push. That takes a couple of minutes.

**2. "How I worked" conflicts with the ADR, which is in the same repo.** The ADR records that I suggested the 20% reserve, and that Option C (the gate) was my proposal. Option C also included a conditional judge, so "I overruled an LLM judge on every request" doesn't match either. The brief says the live conversation tests "only submit what you can defend", and a reviewer who opens the ADR will see the mismatch. You've already heard this from me, so it's your call. If you change anything, **"let q1-3 be as it is"** is the cleanest overrule to defend.

**3. Minor:**
- "Conversation history: Attached in repo" doesn't name the file.
- Line 50 says "The number we couldn't have guessed: The surprising number:…", one label too many.
- `CLAUDE.md` still lists your sections under "Left" as to-dos.

Fixing any of these costs words, and the write-up has almost no room left before page 3.

## Questions to expect in the live conversation
- **"Retries per request is 0.00 in every Table 2b row. Where do retries happen?"** Answer: in a separate experiment (`retry_naive` / `retry_policy` rows in `results.csv`). Real load never hit a 429 that the policy would retry, because admission waits instead.
- **"Why is TPM ≥5,755 rather than 8,000?"** Because `max_tokens` is reserved up front, the tokens actually used before a 429 understate the limit.
- **"50% correct: is that good?"** The ceiling is 75%, since 5 of the 20 questions are unanswerable. The gate let 0 of 14 misread sentences through.
- **"Change the system live: halve TPM"** → `standin.server --tpm-scale 0.5`, then `tests.smoke_pipeline`.

## Why `/export` started on Nov 12
`/export` saves only what's in the session's *current context*. This session ran out of context several times. Each time, Claude Code replaced the earlier messages with a summary, and `/export` only sees what came after the last summary. Nothing is lost, though: Claude Code also writes every message, tool call and result to raw log files on disk.

## Where the complete history is
There are two files in `C:\Users\Abhay JK\.claude\projects\c--Users-Abhay-JK-Downloads-vaani-ai-assignment\`:

| File | Size | Covers |
|---|---|---|
| `95311d7f-82ae-4d1b-ae27-a4a2572ec9e1.jsonl` | 0.34 MB | a short first session, Nov 9 morning |
| `483ae327-5aaa-4f68-aa7e-673cf6d07098.jsonl` | 23 MB | everything else, Nov 9 11:02 to now (this session) |

These are the raw, unedited history: every prompt, reply, tool call and output. Each line is one JSON event, which is hard for people to read.

## How to add it
1. **Copy both files** into a `conversation/` folder in the repo. Do this as the last step, because this session's file keeps growing while we talk.
2. **Replace your email** with `[redacted]`. It appears in the background context Claude Code adds to each session.
3. **Scan for the API key**, both the exact value and any `gsk_` token. Then check that each file is under GitHub's 100 MB limit. At 23 MB they are.
4. **Add a readable version** next to them, `conversation/transcript.md`. It would hold every prompt and reply in order, with tool calls shown as one line each. Say in the README that the `.jsonl` files are the raw record and the `.md` is generated from them, so nobody thinks it's a cleaned-up version.
5. **Keep `agent-convo-history.txt`**, or delete it, since the new files cover it. Point the write-up's "Attached in repo" at `conversation/`, then commit and push.

The commands for step 1, if you want to do it yourself in PowerShell:
```powershell
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force conversation
Copy-Item "$env:USERPROFILE\.claude\projects\c--Users-Abhay-JK-Downloads-vaani-ai-assignment\*.jsonl" conversation\
```
